# E M B L E M S

## XXIII.

->*Draw me: we will run after you at the scent of your perfumes.* <note>This is a translation of the Latin Vulgate from the Song of Solomon 1:4: "Trahe me: post te curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum."</note><-

Draw me, my divine Spouse;
Then we shall run after you:
For the sweet scent of your heavenly perfumes,
By drawing me out of my fatal lethargy, <note>The original "langueurs funestes" refers to a state of spiritual dullness or depression.</note>
Revives me and ravishes my senses:
This fragrance, sweeter than incense,
Invites me ceaselessly to follow you:
Without this divine perfume, I could no longer live.

How much of a novice you still are in your love,
The Spouse replied in his turn:
You desire the attractive sweetness of perfumes;
You are a weak lover!

I know a path more solid and more short;
It is that of my pure love.
There, one seeks neither perfume nor tenderness;
One is guided by Wisdom: <term>Sagesse (Wisdom)</term> <note>In this mystical context, Wisdom refers to the Sophia or the divine intellect that guides the soul beyond mere emotion.</note>

It is there that grief, pain, and torment
Distinguish the perfect Lover.
What! do you wish to walk upon the blooming rose
When I have seen my life end in torments?
Follow me into hardships, expire upon the cross:
Then you shall be worthy of my choice.

<meta>catchword: XXIV. Who</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXIII presents a dialogue based on the Song of Solomon, where the Soul asks to be drawn by divine sweetness, but the Spouse corrects her, stating that true love is proven through suffering and the Cross rather than sensory delights.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Solomon, Divine Spouse, mystical marriage, spiritual perfumes, suffering, the Cross, pure love, Wisdom</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

## XXIII.

<page-type>Illustration</page-type>

<image-desc>A detailed engraving by Jan Smit (marked "I. Smit f." for *fecit*, meaning "made this"). The scene depicts the theology of "Spiritual Attraction." In the foreground, a young, radiant figure representing Divine Love runs forward with purpose, carrying a large, blazing torch that illuminates the path. Behind him, the Soul is shown in a state of humble desperation, lying prostrate on the ground and reaching out toward the light and the scent of the divinity. The setting is a classical garden with a stone terrace and lush trees, symbolizing the "enclosed garden" of the Song of Songs.</image-desc>

->Draw me: we will run after the fragrance of your perfumes. Song of Songs, Chapter 1.<-
<note>original Latin: "Trahe me, post te curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum. Cantic. 1." This verse from the Song of Solomon is a cornerstone of mystical theology, representing the Soul's desire to be pulled toward God by the "scent" of divine grace.</note>

**The Soul:**

Draw me, my divine Spouse;
Then we shall run after you:
For the sweet scent of your heavenly perfumes,
By drawing me out of my fatal lethargy, <note>The original French "langueurs funestes" suggests a state of spiritual melancholy or "acedia" where the soul feels heavy and unable to act.</note>
Revives me and ravishes my senses:
This fragrance, sweeter than incense,
Invites me ceaselessly to follow you:
Without this divine perfume, I could no longer live.

**The Spouse:**

How much of a novice you still are in your love,
The Spouse replied in his turn:
You desire the attractive sweetness of perfumes;
You are a weak lover!

I know a path more solid and more short;
It is that of my pure love.
There, one seeks neither perfume nor tenderness;
One is guided by Wisdom: <term>Wisdom (Sagesse)</term> <note>In this mystical tradition, "Wisdom" refers to the higher spiritual intellect that loves God for Himself, rather than for the "sensible consolations" or "perfumes" of emotional joy.</note>

It is there that grief, pain, and torment
Distinguish the perfect Lover.
What! do you wish to walk upon the blooming rose
When I have seen my life end in torments?
Follow me into hardships, expire upon the cross:
Then you shall be worthy of my choice.

<meta>catchword: XXIV. Who</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXIII illustrates a pivotal moment where the Soul asks to be led by the "perfume" of divine consolation, but the Spouse redirects her toward the "shorter path" of suffering and the Cross as the true mark of perfect love.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Divine Love, spiritual perfumes, lethargy, Wisdom, the Cross, mystical marriage, Jan Smit, divine spouse</keywords>

## XXIV.

<page-type>Illustration</page-type>

<image-desc>A copperplate engraving within a rectangular border, depicting a domestic interior with classical architectural elements, including arched windows and a pedimented doorway. ->O that someone would give you to me as my brother, nursing at my mother’s breasts, so that I might find you outside and kiss you, and then no one would despise me! Song of Songs, Chapter 8.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quis mihi det te fratrem meum sugentem ubera matris meæ, ut inveniam te foris et deosculer te, et jam me nemo despiciat! Cantic. 8." This verse from the Song of Solomon (also known as the Song of Songs) expresses the Soul's longing for a relationship with the Divine that is as intimate as a sibling bond, allowing for public expressions of love without the "despising" or judgment of the world.</note>

**Key Concepts for this Emblem:**

*   **Song of Solomon:** <term>A biblical book of poetry that Christian mystics traditionally interpreted as a dialogue between Christ (the Spouse) and the Human Soul (the Bride).</term>
*   **Jan Smit:** <term>The Dutch engraver responsible for these illustrations, known for his work in the late 17th century.</term>
*   **The Angel:** <term>In this context, the winged figure represents Divine Love or the personification of God's presence.</term>
*   **The Soul:** <term>Represented here as a young child being lifted up; this signifies the "spiritual childhood" where the believer is entirely dependent on divine care.</term>
*   **The Cradle and Baby Walker:** <term>These domestic items symbolize the early stages of spiritual growth, where the Soul is still learning to "walk" in the path of virtue and requires the comfort of its heavenly home.</term>

<summary>Emblem XXIV illustrates the Soul’s yearning for an intimate, familial connection with Divine Love, portrayed through the metaphor of a brotherly embrace. The domestic setting emphasizes a state of spiritual infancy and the desire to express one's faith openly without fear of social shame.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Songs, Divine Love, Anima, Jan Smit, spiritual childhood, intimacy, domestic allegory</keywords>

# BY H. HUGO <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest and author of the "Pia Desideria," a famous book of "emblem" poems about the soul's journey toward God.</note>

### XXIV.

->*Who will give you to me, O my brother, sucking the*<-
->*breasts of my mother, so that I may find you*<-
->*outside, and that I may give you a kiss, and that*<-
->*in the future no one may despise me!*<-

AH, who will give me my Brother,
->Who sucks the breast of my mother!<-
->That I may carry him upon my heart,<-
->That I may embrace him with fervor!<-
If he favors me with his chaste kisses
->I no longer fear that I will be despised:<-
->For I wish to lead him outside;<-
->There everyone shall see my raptures.*<-
<note>The term "transports" (raptures) refers to an ecstatic state of spiritual joy.</note>
Divine child, author of my long suffering,
->You revive my hope;<-
I find you now; what an excess of pleasure!
->I will set my desire before you;<-
It is to see myself united with you without division:
->Grant me this advantage,<-
->Then I shall fear nothing more,<-
Peaceful possessor of my only good.

# EMBLEMS

### XXV.

> *I sought him whom my soul loves in my little bed during the nights: I sought him; and I found him not.* <note>A translation of Song of Solomon 3:1. The author uses the "little bed" as a metaphor for spiritual laziness or worldly comfort.</note>

Why do you seek in bed
->Your Spouse, indiscreet Lover?<- <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, and the "Lover" to the human soul, a common theme in mystical poetry.</note>
In vain you seek him there in this dark night;
->He does not make his retreat there.<-

Step forward a little, there he is upon the Cross
->Pierced with nails, adorned with thorns;<-
You will never find him except upon this wood,
Sorrows and pains are his divine paths.

It is quite in vain that we seek
Jesus in the repose of an unworthy softness: <term>mollesse (softness)</term> <note>In this context, it refers to a life of ease, luxury, or spiritual apathy.</note>
->Never shall we find him there;<-
He lives in sorrow, he dies in sadness:
->He tires himself incessantly<-
->To win over the sinful soul;<-
->His rest is found in torment.<-

Let us suffer, let us die to everything; we shall find without
->difficulty,<-
->The illustrious Spouse of our heart.<-
->It is a very vain search<-
To wish to find our Savior in bed.

<meta>catchword: XXVI. I</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXV reflects on Song of Solomon 3:1, arguing that Christ (the Spouse) cannot be found in a life of comfort or "softness," but only through the suffering of the Cross. It encourages the soul to embrace pain and self-denial to achieve true union with the Savior.</summary>

Sorrows and pains are his divine paths.

It is quite in vain that we seek
Jesus in the repose of an unworthy softness: <term>mollesse (softness)</term> <note>In this context, "softness" refers to a life of ease, luxury, or spiritual apathy that prevents a person from truly seeking God.</note>
->Never shall we find him there;<-
He lives in sorrow, he dies in sadness:
->He tires himself incessantly<-
->To win over the sinful soul;<- <gloss>anima</gloss>
->His rest is found in torment.<-

Let us suffer, let us die to everything; we shall find without
->difficulty,<-
->The illustrious Spouse of our heart.<-
->It is a very vain search<-
To wish to find our Savior in bed.

<meta>catchword: XXVI. I</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXV reflects on the soul's search for Christ, arguing that the Savior cannot be found in a life of ease or comfort (symbolized by the "little bed" from Song of Solomon), but only through the sacrifice of the Cross.</summary>
<keywords>Emblems, Song of Solomon, Christ as Spouse, Divine Love, The Cross, Spiritual Discipline, Suffering, Mysticism</keywords>

# OF H. HUGO.

### X X V I.

> *I will rise now, and go about the city in the streets, and in the broad ways I will seek him whom my soul loveth: I sought him, but I found him not.* <note>This is a translation of the Latin Vulgate text of Song of Solomon 3:2.</note>

No, no, I no longer wish to live in repose,
I want to run everywhere seeking the one I love:
I have sought him inopportunely,
Never again will I do the same.
Of a great city I shall make the circuit
To bear witness to him of my love.

->What are you doing, O foolish Lover?<-

Ah, how poorly you seek, always at the wrong time!
You follow nothing but your own inclination,
And let yourself be guided by the senses.
You seek in the bed; Jesus is on the Cross:
He is near to you; yet you run through the city.
You are mistaken in your choice:
Do not leave this little dwelling <note>The "little domicile" refers to the interior of the heart or the cell of contemplation.</note>.
Love, suffer for him, who will take your heart,
In order to make it his retreat:
Then you will be satisfied,
At all times, in all places possessing this happiness.
You will taste peace even in suffering,
You will desire nothing more;
And your heart, content to possess this good,
You will have all the rest in superabundance.

<meta>XXVII. No. 4.</meta>

<keywords>Herman Hugo, Song of Solomon, spiritual search, interiority, suffering, Divine Love, mysticism</keywords>

### X X V I I.

> *Have you not seen him whom my soul loves?* <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 3:3-4.</note>
> *When I had passed a little beyond them,*
> *I found him whom my soul loves: I hold him;*
> *and I will let him go no more.*

BY distancing myself from every creature <note>In mystical theology, "creature" refers to any created thing or person other than God.</note>
I have found my celestial Spouse:
When I followed my nature too closely
I deprived myself of a blessing so sweet.

I hold him, this faithful Lover,
I will no longer suffer him to stray from me;
I swear to him today an eternal love
And forever an inviolable faith.

Let us remain, dear Spouse, in this solitude,
I will reveal my fires <note>The "fires" of spiritual passion or divine love.</note> to you:
I will suffer no dark anxiety there:
To possess you is the goal of my vows.

There, separated and far from all things
I will tell you of my loves:
Ah, grant that my heart may rest in your heart,
And that it may rest there always!

# O F H. H U G O.

## X X V I I I.

> *->But for me, all my good is to keep myself united<-*
> *->to God, and to place all my hope<-*
> *->in the Lord, my God.<-* <note>original: "Mais pour moi, tout mon bien est de me tenir uni à Dieu..." This is a paraphrase of Psalm 73:28.</note>

How good it is for me to cling to you,
And to place my confidence there!
Is there anything, my divine Spouse, <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride of God.</note>
More charming than this adherence?
There our hearts are united, we have but one will,
My hope is not in vain;
I experience the divine power,
Which is pleased to carry me with a sovereign hand.
I no longer fear pain or danger,
Carried as I am by this God whom I adore.
How light the torment appears!
I love Him all the more, O pleasure, as I abhor you.
What a change, great God, I discover in my heart!
I used to love vanity, now I see it as detestable:
I used to fear the slightest pain;
Now torment appears lovely to me.
It is you, divine Love, who have done this good for me,
For without you I could do nothing.

# E M B L E M S

### XXIX.

->*I rested under the shadow of him whom I had so greatly desired.*<-
<note>This is a paraphrase of Song of Solomon 2:3: "I sat down under his shadow with great delight."</note>

Alas, what pains and labors I have suffered!
I was wandering and a vagabond,
I found nothing in the world
That could serve to soothe my ills.
Happily, I have found upon this wood
<note>The "wood" refers to the Cross of Christ, a common motif in 17th-century mystical poetry.</note>
The one whom my soul loves:
By an extreme happiness,
My heart has made this worthy choice.
I have found my rest beneath this fertile tree,
Where love holds him bound;
I have chosen it for my dwelling,
My heart can no longer be detached from it.
I rest beneath his shadow,
It is where I live both night and day:
The more my dwelling appears somber,
The more it has what is needed to please my Love.
There I find fruits of an exquisite sweetness;
Others would find them bitter:
For me, I confess with frankness
That I have seen none such in the universe.

<meta>catchword: XXX. Com-</meta>

<summary>In this emblem, the soul reflects on its transition from a weary wanderer to finding eternal rest and spiritual nourishment beneath the "shadow" of the Beloved on the Cross. It emphasizes that the hardships of the divine life, though appearing bitter or dark to the world, are sweet to the devoted heart.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual rest, the Cross, Song of Solomon, divine love, mystical union, wandering soul, spiritual fruit</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XXIX.

->*I sat under the shadow of him whom I had desired.*<-
->*Song of Solomon, Chapter 2.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Sub umbra illius quem desideraveram, sedi. Cantic. 2." This refers to Song of Solomon 2:3: "I sat down under his shadow with great delight, and his fruit was sweet to my taste."</note>

Alas, what pains and labors I have suffered!
I was wandering and a vagabond,
I found nothing in the world
That could serve to soothe my ills.

Happily, I have found upon this wood
<note>The "wood" refers to the Cross of Christ, a common motif in 17th-century mystical poetry where the Cross is depicted as a fertile "Tree of Life."</note>
The one whom my soul loves:
By an extreme happiness,
My heart has made this worthy choice.

I have found my rest beneath this fertile tree,
Where love holds him bound;
I have chosen it for my dwelling,
My heart can no longer be detached from it.

I rest beneath his shadow,
It is where I live both night and day:
The more my dwelling appears somber,
The more it has what is needed to please my Love.
<note>In mystical tradition, the "shadow" or "darkness" of the Cross is paradoxically the place of greatest spiritual light and intimacy.</note>

There I find fruits of an exquisite sweetness;
Others would find them bitter:
For me, I confess with frankness
That I have seen none such in the universe.

<meta>catchword: XXX. Com-</meta>

<summary>In this emblem, the soul reflects on its transition from a weary wanderer to finding eternal rest and spiritual nourishment beneath the "shadow" of the Beloved on the Cross. It emphasizes that the hardships of the divine life, though appearing bitter or dark to the world, are sweet to the devoted heart.</summary>

### XXX.

->*How shall we sing the Lord’s song*<-
->*in a foreign land? Psalm 136.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Quomodo cantabimus canticum Domini in terra aliena? Pfal. 136." This refers to Psalm 137:4 in most modern Bibles (Psalm 136 in the Latin Vulgate). Historically, this text describes the Israelites weeping by the rivers of Babylon, unable to sing their sacred songs in a land of captivity.</note>

<note>The illustration shows the soul in a state of melancholy. The abandoned lute and horn signify that worldly music and earthly pleasures have lost their charm or are insufficient for the soul's current state of spiritual longing. The angel, representing divine grace or the Word of God, offers a book of sacred songs, yet the soul looks away, overwhelmed by the "foreign land" of earthly existence.</note>

### XXX.

->*How could we sing the songs of the*<-
->*Lord in a foreign land?*<-
<note>original: "Quomodo cantabimus canticum Domini in terra aliena?" from Psalm 137:4. This refers to the Babylonian Captivity, used here as a metaphor for the soul's exile from Heaven.</note>

->*The Soul.*<-

How could I, alas, in this foreign land
->Still strike up holy melodies?<-
When I was near you, my Lord and my
->Father,<-
I composed sacred concerts.
->Now I leave my lyre behind,<-
I can no longer strike up songs:
->I must, I must sigh;<-
My sad heart has no more music left.

->*Our Lord.*<-

It is I, it is I, who wishes that for my
->glory<-
You may sing in every place:
For there is no dwelling-place so dark,
Where one ought not to love and burn with my fires.

->*The Soul.*<-

Let us sing then, dear Spouse: how beautiful the harmony is
->When two hearts are truly in love,<-
And their flame is chaste and faithful,
How marvelous is this accord!
->It is a concert that is always the same,<-
One finds no false notes there,
->Never does one perceive a "No":<-
What one desires, when love is extreme,
->The other answers at that very instant:<-
Never a different note:
->Oh, how charming is this Canticle,<-
Which denotes divine Love!
Let us sing, my heart, both night and day:
One cannot sing too much when one is full of love.

<sig>L I-</sig>

<summary>A poetic dialogue between the Soul and Christ based on Psalm 137, where the Soul's initial mourning in earthly exile is transformed into a harmonious song of divine love. The text emphasizes the perfect mystical union between the believer and God, characterized by a "concert" without discord.</summary>

<keywords>Herman Hugo, Pia Desideria, Divine Love, Mystical Marriage, Psalm 137, Spiritual Exile, Sacred Music, Devotion</keywords>

# BOOK III.

## XXXI.

> *I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you*
> *find my Beloved, tell him that*
> *I languish with love.* <note>A direct reference to the Song of Solomon 5:8, a common source for mystical emblems.</note>

O You, whom I perceive, my faithful Companions,
->You who travel through the countryside,<-
->If you should meet one day<-
My Spouse <note>original: "Epoux"; representing Christ or the Divine</note>, tell him that I languish with love.

->Alas! I have run just as you have<-
->To meet the one I love:<-
->All my labors seemed sweet to me<-
->In order to find this lovely Spouse:<-
But at present my weakness is extreme.
My heart is pierced by his divine charms,
->And I no longer know how to take a step,<-
I find my rest in the love that enchants me;
->And this rest reduces me to the last gasp.<-
Alas! I would cease to be so listless
If I could hear once more his adorable voice.

->Tell him that I am dying;<-
Paint for him my torment, O my lovely sisters:
->Inform him that his Beloved,<- <note>original: "Amante"; the soul personified as a female lover</note>
Is ready to expire under the weight of her sorrows.

# BOOK III.

## XXXII.

> Sustain me with flowers, surround me with apples, for
> I languish with love. <note>original Latin: "Fulcite me floribus, stipate me malis, quia amore langueo." A quotation from the Song of Solomon 2:5 (also known as the Canticle of Canticles). In mystical tradition, these "fruits" and "flowers" represent the virtues or the consolations of the Holy Spirit that support the soul when she is overwhelmed by God's presence.</note> *Song of Solomon 2.*

<term>Song of Solomon</term> <note>A book of the Bible consisting of love poetry, historically interpreted by theologians as an allegory for the relationship between God (the Groom) and the Human Soul or the Church (the Bride).</note>

<term>Amore langueo</term> <note>Latin for "I languish with love." This refers to a state of spiritual ecstasy so powerful that the body becomes weak, a common theme in the writings of mystics like St. Teresa of Avila.</note>

<vocab>Song of Solomon, Jacobus Smit, I languish with love, pastoral, apples, flowers</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates the spiritual exhaustion of the Soul, who has become "sick with love" for the Divine. It depicts her being comforted by companions with symbolic fruit and flowers, representing the need for spiritual refreshment during the intense journey of devotion.</summary>

### XXXII.

->*Sustain me with flowers, strengthen me with apples: because I languish with love.*<- <note>original: "Soutenez moi avec des fleurs, fortifiez moi avec des pommes : parce que je languis d'amour." This is a translation of Song of Solomon 2:5.</note>

---

Alas, I am dying! Ah, cover me with flowers,
        Do not abandon me, my sisters,
        Surround me with those apples
        Found in the garden of the Bridegroom:
        Ah, hide me from all men;
        And let me be alone with you.

"Of what use, incomparable Lover,
"Are these apples and these flowers? You are languishing;
"You require the celestial favors of love:
        "Do you fear a lack of flowers?
                "It is no longer these trifles <term>bagatelles: small, insignificant things</term>
                "Which must now bring you relief:
"Thorns and crosses, these are the new flowers
        "Which the Bridegroom wishes to share with you.
                "Leave the savory apple behind;
                "You must become generous <note>In this context, "generous" implies a nobility of spirit and a willingness to suffer for God.</note>
        "If you wish to please the celestial Bridegroom;
"This is the means to draw him into you."

<vocab>H. Hugo, Song of Songs, Bridegroom, Lover, apples, flowers, trifles, thorns, crosses, relieve, attract</vocab>

<summary>A poetic meditation on Song of Solomon 2:5, where the Soul asks for the comfort of flowers and fruit, but is told by a divine voice that true union with the Bridegroom comes through the 'flowers' of suffering—thorns and crosses.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Song of Solomon, Divine Love, mysticism, spiritual suffering, Bridegroom and Bride, asceticism</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XXXIII.

> *My Beloved is mine, and I am his. He feeds among the lilies, until the day begins to break, and the shadows gradually flee away.* <note>This is a translation of Song of Solomon 2:16-17, a central text in Christian mysticism representing the soul's union with God.</note>

It is finished, it is finished; I desire flowers no more,
->Unless it be to weave a crown<-
For my celestial Spouse; and for him I abandon
->From this moment on such tasteless sweets.<-

->The gathering of lilies surrounding me<-
->Represents my purity;<-
->And it is my Spouse who bestows it;<-
Whatever is not from him is nothing but vanity. <note>original: "vanité" — in this context, meaning emptiness or lack of spiritual substance.</note>

Dear and divine Spouse, ah, keep your favors;
What you give to me, I wish to return at once:
->These flowers are not enough,<-
My heart is entirely yours, never to be taken back.

->We shall rejoice in the midst of these lilies,<-
->Until the day returns;<-
->Delightful are my nights,<-
When you permit me then to converse with you!

If I am entirely yours, you are entirely mine,
->My happiness, my joy is extreme,<-
->Love is my only law;<-
You love me: You know, Lord, that I love you.

<term>Emblem</term> (A symbolic picture accompanied by a motto and a poetic reflection), <term>Spouse</term> (In mystical tradition, Christ is the Bridegroom and the human soul is the Bride), <term>Lilies</term> (Symbols of purity and the presence of the Divine), <term>Vanity</term> (The fleeting nature of worldly things), <term>Love</term>, <term>Purity</term>, <term>Lord</term>

# EMBLEMS

### XXXIII.

> *My Beloved is mine, and I am his; he feeds among the lilies, until the day begins to break, and the shadows gradually flee away.* <note>original: "Dilectus meus mihi et ego illi qui pascitur inter lilia; donec aspiret dies et inclinentur umbræ." This is a translation of Song of Solomon 2:16-17, a foundational text in Christian mysticism representing the "Spiritual Marriage" between the soul and God.</note>

It is finished, it is finished; I desire flowers no more,
->Unless it be to weave a crown<-
For my celestial Spouse; and for him I abandon
->From this moment on such tasteless sweets.<- <note>The speaker rejects worldly pleasures ("tasteless sweets") in favor of a spiritual crown for Christ.</note>

The gathering of lilies surrounding me
->Represents my purity;<-
->And it is my Spouse who bestows it;<-
Whatever is not from him is nothing but vanity. <note>original: "vanité" — in this context, meaning emptiness or the fleeting, illusory nature of the material world compared to the eternal.</note>

Dear and divine Spouse, ah, keep your favors;
What you give to me, I wish to return at once:
->These flowers are not enough,<-
My heart is entirely yours, never to be taken back.

->We shall rejoice in the midst of these lilies,<-
->Until the day returns;<-
->Delightful are my nights,<-
When you permit me then to converse with you! <note>A reference to the "Dark Night" of the soul, which becomes delightful when illuminated by divine presence.</note>

If I am entirely yours, you are entirely mine,
->My happiness, my joy is extreme,<-
->Love is my only law;<-
You love me: You know, Lord, that I love you.

->Song of Solomon 7<-

[The engraving for this emblem, created by the artist I. Smith, depicts an allegorical scene within a rectangular border. In the foreground, two figures stand in a natural landscape. On the left is a winged, haloed youth representing Divine Love, wearing a short tunic. On the right, a woman representing the Soul in a long gown holds out a shallow bowl containing a magnetic compass. A bright ray of light extends from the youth's chest to the needle of the compass. In the top left corner, the sun radiates light from behind clouds. In the background on the right, a large sunflower grows, its bloom turned towards the sun. The scene is set against a backdrop of distant hills.]

<note>This illustration employs two popular 17th-century symbols for spiritual devotion: the <term>heliotrope</term> (sunflower), which physically turns to follow the sun, and the magnetic compass. Here, Divine Love acts as the "pole" or the "sun," exerting a spiritual magnetism that ensures the Soul remains constant in her direction.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the mystical concept of "conversion," using the visual metaphors of a sunflower and a magnetic compass to show how the Soul is naturally and irresistibly drawn to God. It marks a shift from the soul's possession of the Divine to its total orientation toward the Divine will.</summary>

### XXXIV.

->*I am my Beloved’s, and his heart turns toward me.* <note>A paraphrase of Song of Solomon 7:10: "I am my beloved's, and his desire is toward me."</note><-

My heart follows you everywhere, O my divine Lover,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Just as iron follows its magnet:
You mark upon my heart, as if upon a compass,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;By your glances, by your word,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Your adorable will,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And you turn me in every direction.

The Heliotrope <note>The sunflower or "turn-sole," a common symbol in mystical literature for the soul that always turns toward God, the Sun of Justice.</note> also turns toward the light
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of its Sun, with whom it is in love;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And being unable to leave the earth,
It wishes, like the sun, to make the circuit of the heavens.

My heart, thus converted toward love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Finds that love is its living light:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It guides me in my career <note>In this context, "career" refers to one's path or course through life.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It creates both my night and my day.

If he moves away from me, I am in darkness;
When he is near me, the night becomes brightness:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He inspires me with his truth;
Without him, all objects are but mournful things.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without him, I would be in death;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He is within me the spirit and the life;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From all evils I am set free
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without making any effort of my own.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**HE IS MINE, I AM HIS;**
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How reciprocal is this love!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nothing in this is ambiguous
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since he is its firm support.

<summary>This poem uses the metaphors of a compass needle and a heliotrope flower to describe the soul's total orientation toward the Divine. It emphasizes a mystical reciprocity where the soul finds life and light only through the presence of the Beloved.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Song of Solomon, Heliotrope, Compass, Mysticism, Herman Hugo, Soul's Journey</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XXXV.

->*My soul melted as soon as my Beloved spoke.*<-
<note>A reference to Song of Solomon 5:6. In mystical tradition, the "Beloved" represents God or Christ, and the "soul" represents the individual seeker.</note>

O pure and divine Fire, delightful warmth,
You destroy a soul filled with love!
I melt as soon as I hear the sweetness
Of that divine word:
It is this word that dissolves my heart:
What a marvelous school love is!
The soul flows away into her Lord.

She no longer retains any consistency of her own;
She loses herself and is engulfed in her God:
The activity of such a beautiful fire
Grants her a complete innocence.

It is you, divine Love, who works this change;
It is you who makes the soul pass into what she loves;
It is you who reduces her to a certain nothingness,
Where she finds the All through an extreme happiness.
->Let us banish all sense of self-ownership,<-
<note>The original term "propriété" refers to the spiritual obstacle of "self-appropriation" or the ego's claim over its own actions and will.</note>
->We shall find the truth;<-
->And we shall find it within the very source.<-

### XXXVI.

->*For what is there for me in heaven, and what do I desire on earth, if not you?* <note>original: "Car qu’y a-t’il pour moi dans le ciel, & que désirai-je sur la terre, si non vous?" This is a translation of Psalm 73:25.</note><-

After this transformation, what could I possibly want
    On the earth or even in heaven?
I find within myself neither desire nor power;
    Everything has passed into the One I love.

You are, O my God, the heaven of heavens for me,
    Your happiness makes me content;
    You will always be glorious,
    I have, therefore, no further expectation.

All my good is in you, it cannot perish;
    You will always be worthy of worship:
    This is where my desire finds its limit;
Your felicity makes mine immutable <note>unchanging and eternal</note>.

O my celestial Spouse, I cannot express
    What I feel in the depths of my soul:
    You have deigned to imprint it
    With strokes of pure flame.
    Ah, never erase them;
    It is the height of my wishes!

<summary>A devotional poem expressing the soul's complete union with God, where earthly and heavenly desires are replaced by the joy of divine glory. It reflects the "Spouse" imagery common in mystical traditions, portraying the soul as a bride to the celestial bridegroom.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Pia Desideria, Psalm 73, divine love, celestial spouse, mysticism, spiritual transformation, 17th-century devotion</keywords>

### XXXVII.

->*Alas, how long is my exile! I live among the*<-
->*inhabitants of Kedar <note>Kedar: A biblical reference to a nomadic tribe, often used to symbolize a place of spiritual desolation or distance from God.</note>. My soul is a*<-

->I await the end of my journey;<-
->And your divine light<-

->Whose inhabitants I abhor;<-

->Which redoubles my torments.<-

->Yet I dwell among them,<-

->You have deigned to inflame me;<-

->Since I live only for you?<-
->Ah, if ever my heart was faithful to you<-
->Carry me away, my dear Spouse!<-

**Technical Vocabulary**

<term>Skeleton</term> — The bony framework of the body. In emblem books, the skeleton represents the end of the earthly journey and the transition of the soul.

<term>Anatomy</term> — The study of the structure of living things. Here, the anatomical figure serves both a scientific and a moral purpose, showing the internal reality of all humans regardless of status.

# XXXVIII.

<note>The engraving on this page serves as a visual metaphor for the biblical text below it. A large skeleton sits in a desolate landscape, its ribcage forming the bars of a cell. Inside this "body of death," a living person kneels in prayer, desperately seeking liberation. The skeleton's melancholic pose—resting its head on its hand—mirrors the philosophical "Melencolia" tradition, suggesting a mournful reflection on the human condition.</note>

->*Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death? From Romans 7.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Infelix ego homo! Quis me liberabit de corpore mortis huius? Ad Rom. 7." This is a famous passage from the Epistle to the Romans (7:24), where the Apostle Paul describes the conflict between the spiritual will and the physical "flesh."</note>

**Technical Vocabulary**

<term>Wretched man that I am</term> — (Latin: *Infelix ego homo*) A cry of spiritual distress, often used in devotional literature to express the soul's frustration with its own failings and physical limitations.

<term>Will deliver</term> — (Latin: *liberabit*) To set free or rescue. In this context, it refers to the divine intervention required to release the soul from its earthly cage.

<term>Body of death</term> — (Latin: *corpore mortis*) A striking metaphor for the mortal human body, which is seen as subject to decay and a hindrance to the eternal life of the soul.

<term>Romans 7</term> — (Latin: *Ad Romanos 7*) The seventh chapter of St. Paul's letter to the Romans, a foundational text in Christian theology regarding sin, the law, and the struggle for salvation.

<summary>This page illustrates a literal interpretation of Romans 7:24, depicting a living man imprisoned within the ribcage of a skeleton to represent the soul's confinement within the mortal body.</summary>
<keywords>Romans 7:24, body as prison, mortality, skeleton, deliverance, spiritual struggle, Memento Mori</keywords>

# BY HERMAN HUGO.

### XXXVIII.

> ->*Wretched man that I am! who will deliver me*<-
> ->*from the body of this death?*<-
<note>original: "Infelix ego homo, quis me liberabit de corpore mortis huius?" — a direct quotation from the Apostle Paul in Romans 7:24.</note>

I languish here within a prison,
Where I might, dear Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Soul as the Bride.</note>, turn against you:
Ah, behold my affliction,
And prevent me from displeasing you.

I am, alas, I am a wretched man,
Still imprisoned within myself,
Who does nothing noble or generous
To please this object of my adoration and love.

The spirit draws me upward; the body pulls me down;
For me, it is a strange combat:
I wish to walk in your footsteps;
And yet, despite myself, my body subjects me to its desires.

Have pity, Great God, upon my unhappy fate;
You know my extreme weakness:
Deliver me from this body of death;
I await this from your wisdom.

<meta>catchword: XXXIX. I</meta>

<summary>A poetic paraphrase of Romans 7:24, where the author laments the internal conflict between spiritual aspirations and physical desires, personifying the soul as a prisoner seeking deliverance from God.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Romans 7:24, spiritual combat, body and soul, divine spouse, prison of the body, repentance</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### X X X I X.

->*I find myself pressed from both sides: for I desire*<-
->*to be released from the bonds of the body, and to be*<-
->*with Jesus Christ.*<-
<note>This is a paraphrase of Philippians 1:23: "I am torn between the two: I desire to depart and be with Christ, which is better by far."</note>

My heart flies toward you; my body clings to the
earth;
Break then this bond that holds it attached;
Since you alone are able to do it;
Against my prayer <note>original: "oraison"</note> do not be angry.
O you, Lord in whom I hope,
By my sorrow be touched,
You are my Lord, my Savior, and my Father.

I ardently desire, in order to unite with you,
To be very far from all the rest:
You know, my divine Spouse,
How much I detest this world.

I am here, however, despite myself,
And I remain here in patience:
Your will shall always be my law,
I shall live by obedience.

<summary>Emblem 39 expresses the soul's internal conflict between its earthly existence and its desire for spiritual union with Christ. The speaker petitions God to release them from physical bonds while vowing to remain patient and obedient to the divine will.</summary>

### XXXIX.

->*I find myself pressed from both sides: for I desire*<-
->*to be released from the bonds of the body, and to be*<-
->*with Jesus Christ.*<-

My heart flies toward you; my body clings to the earth;
Break then this bond that holds it fastened;
Since you alone are able to do it;
Against my prayer <note>original: "oraison," meaning a formal or heartfelt prayer</note> do not be angry.
O you, Lord in whom I hope,
By my sorrow be touched,
You are my Lord, my Savior, and my Father.

I ardently desire, in order to unite with you,
To be very far from all the rest:
You know, my divine Spouse <note>A mystical reference to Christ as the bridegroom of the Soul</note>,
How much I detest this world.

I am here, however, despite myself,
And I remain here in patience:
Your will shall always be my law,
I shall live by obedience.

<summary>Emblem 39 illustrates the spiritual tension of the soul "chained" to the physical world while yearning for heaven. It uses the metaphor of a bird on a string to show that while the spirit may soar in prayer, it remains tied to the weight of mortality until God chooses to release it.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual longing, body and soul, Divine Love, Philippians 1:23, obedience, mortality, mystical marriage</keywords>

# OF HERMAN HUGO
<note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest and author of the famous emblem book 'Pia Desideria', which this text reflects.</note>

### XL

> *Bring my soul out of prison, that I may praise your Name.*
<note>original: "Educ de custodia animam meam ad confitendum nomini tuo." Psalm 142:7</note>

**A**las, my soul is a prisoner!
You could, dear Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride.</note>, draw her from this prison:
But you do not hear my prayer;
I am left in utter confusion.
Ah, if by your goodness you would draw me out of *myself*,
That would be a double advantage;
For the *self* is nothing but a slavery,
Which renders me unworthy of you.
Divine Spouse, sweet center of my soul,
Ah! it is against this *self* that I cry out without end;
For that is the prison too fatal to my heart:
The other <insert>physical prison</insert> can be borne with patience:
Draw me out of *myself*, dear Victor,
And I shall live, though in suffering,
Without complaining of my misfortune.

<summary>A poetic meditation on Psalm 142, where the author pleads with Christ to liberate the soul not from a physical cell, but from the spiritual bondage of the ego and self-will.</summary>
<keywords>soul's prison, spiritual marriage, self-denial, Herman Hugo, Jesuit spirituality, divine spouse</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, following the previous meditations on the soul's journey toward the Divine.</meta>

# EMBLEMS

### XLI.

->As the deer pants with longing for the water springs; so my soul pants for you, O my God. <note>A translation of Psalm 42:1, a common motif in devotional literature representing the soul's thirst for spiritual fulfillment.</note><-

The deer desires with far less fervor
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The clear waters of a fountain,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Than I desire, O Lord,
The water you promised to the Samaritan woman. <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 4:10-14, where Jesus offers "living water" that quenches thirst eternally.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Do not let me languish any longer,
My thirst <note>original: "altération," meaning a state of being parched or changed by thirst</note> has become extreme:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You know how much I love you,
I cannot delay this happiness without dying.

Give me in my thirst those inexhaustible waters,
Which produce in us a river full of peace:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Your kindnesses are inexhaustible,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Deign to satisfy my wishes.

By quenching my thirst you will restore my life:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ah, take pity on my fate:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since I am enslaved to you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Come, or give me death.

<meta>Catchword: XLII. When</meta>

<summary>This page presents a poetic meditation on spiritual longing, using the biblical imagery of a thirsty deer and the Samaritan woman to express the soul's desperate need for divine grace. It emphasizes the theme of "living water" as the only source of true peace and life.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual thirst, Psalm 42, Samaritan woman, living water, divine love, devotion, emblems</keywords>

<term>Shadow demonstration</term> <note>A method of showing how an object placed between a light source and a screen creates a silhouette. This was often used as an allegory for the "shadows" of the material world compared to the "light" of the spiritual realm.</note>

## XLII.

->*When shall I come and appear before the face of God?*<-
<note>Original Latin: "Quando veniam et apparebo ante faciem Dei?" from Psalm 42:2.</note>

When will you grant me this grace
Of calling me to be near you?
When will it be, O divine Spouse,
That you make my happiness complete?
When will you let me see your lovely face?
I languish night and day:
If you accept my love,
Release me from this slavery <note>The "slavery" refers to the mortal body or the earthly exile of the soul.</note>.

You are my sovereign Good,
My happiness, my center, and my glory:
Outside of you I desire nothing;
You have won an entire victory over my heart.

Do you wish to leave me languishing long,
Author of my modest flame?
Do you wish to leave me groaning long?
You attract me, you carry away my soul:
One would be too happy from this strong attraction,
If one could die, and die before your eyes!

O too-happy Lover, ah how beautiful is your lot!
What, do you believe yourself unfortunate?
To secure your destiny
The Spouse would only have to draw back the curtain <note>A metaphor for the veil between life and death, or the revelation of the divine presence.</note>.

But you do not understand this august mystery;
If you knew how to find Him through faith,
Far from aspiring to your final hour,
You would abandon yourself to the will of your King.

That which one believes to be an extreme love,
Still curves back upon itself <note>A reference to "incurvatus in se," the theological concept of love being secretly self-serving rather than purely focused on God.</note>;
One wants to enjoy the Object of one's affection:
But perfect resignation
Into the hands of God pleases Him in His subject.
He is not honored by everything we wish for:
The wish is the effect of our own will;
And one must hand everything over to His pure goodness.

<summary>This page features a poetic dialogue where the soul first expresses a passionate desire to die and be united with God, but is then corrected by a voice advising that true love requires patient resignation to God's will.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Pia Desideria, Divine Spouse, longing for death, mystical union, resignation, self-love vs divine love, faith</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, following the previous meditation on divine longing. The catchword "Sei-" likely refers to "Seigneur" (Lord) which begins the next section.</meta>

### XLIII.

->*Who will give me wings like those of a dove; and I will fly away, and find rest?* <note>A translation of Psalm 55:6, a common motif in mystical literature representing the soul's desire to escape the physical world.</note><-

Give to me, my divine Spouse, <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride of God.</note>
Wings like those of the dove,
So that I may fly toward you,
That my loves may be eternal.
My spirit and my heart are no longer on the earth,
They already inhabit the celestial dwelling:
Destroy, O divine Love,
This heavy body which confines me.
It is this which still holds me back,
My soul is already in the heavens;
Ah, grant, Lord whom I adore,
That I may expire before your eyes!
I am in extreme pain,
And in a state of agitation;
Draw me out, since I love you,
And call me toward you; O Lord of Zion.
There I shall taste you in a profound peace,
Which is hardly known here below.
Happy is he who, separated from the world,
Occupies himself night and day with your divine charms! <term>charms: originally "apas" (appas), referring to the attractive power or spiritual beauty of the divine.</term>

<summary>This emblem reflects on Psalm 55, expressing the mystic's intense desire to shed the "heavy body" and fly toward a union with the divine Spouse. It emphasizes the soul's restlessness on earth and its longing for the peace found only in the celestial realm.</summary>
<keywords>mysticism, divine spouse, soul's flight, Psalm 55, spiritual longing, Zion, peace</keywords>

### XLIII.

->*Who will give me wings like those of a dove; and I will fly away, and find rest?* <note>original: "Quis dabit mihi pennas sicut columba, et volabo et requiescam?" This is Psalm 55:6 (numbered as Psalm 54 in the Latin Vulgate). It is a central motif in Christian mysticism representing the "Anima" or Soul seeking to escape the "cage" of the physical body.</note><-

Give to me, my divine Spouse, <note>In mystical tradition, the "Spouse" refers to Christ, drawing on the imagery of the Song of Solomon where the relationship between God and the soul is depicted as a sacred marriage.</note>
Wings like those of the dove,
So that I may fly toward you,
That my loves may be eternal.
My spirit and my heart are no longer on the earth,
They already inhabit the celestial dwelling:
Destroy, O divine Love,
This heavy body which confines me.
It is this which still holds me back,
My soul is already in the heavens;
Ah, grant, Lord whom I adore,
That I may expire before your eyes! <note>The word "expire" here carries the double meaning of physical death and the "mystical death," where the self is completely lost in God.</note>
I am in extreme pain,
And in a state of agitation;
Draw me out, since I love you,
And call me toward you, O Lord of Zion.
There I shall taste you in a profound peace,
Which is hardly known here below.
Happy is he who, separated from the world,
Occupies himself night and day with your divine charms! <term>charms: originally "appas" (attractions), a term used in 17th-century spirituality to describe the irresistible beauty and goodness of God that draws the soul upward.</term>

<summary>This emblem illustrates Psalm 55, depicting the winged Soul's intense longing to shed its earthly "heavy body" and fly to a state of eternal rest in the divine. It emphasizes the mystical desire for "Zion"—a state of profound peace found only in union with the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 55, wings of a dove, divine spouse, mystical death, Zion, spiritual longing, soul's flight</keywords>

# XIX.

<note>The image and text on this page are extremely faded, appearing as a faint "ghost" print or heavily worn engraving. This may be due to the age of the paper or the specific printing technique used in this edition.</note>

<note>The engraving shows a landscape where a person—representing the soul or the Psalmist—is kneeling on the left side. They are looking up with raised hands toward a celestial light breaking through the clouds. To the right, there is a large, leafy tree, providing a natural setting for this moment of prayer.</note>

->As the deer longs for the springs of water:<-
->so my soul longs for you, O God. Psalm 41.<-
<note>original: "Sicut cervus desiderat ad fontes aquarum: ita desiderat anima mea ad te Deus. Psal. 41." This verse is known as the "Sicut Cervus." While the author labels it Psalm 41 according to the Latin Vulgate, it is found as Psalm 42:1 in modern English Bibles.</note>

<term>Psalm 41</term> <note>Following the Greek and Latin numbering; most modern Protestant and Hebrew-based Bibles list this as Psalm 42.</note>, <term>deer</term> <note>Latin: cervus; in medieval and early modern art, the deer was a symbol of the faithful soul seeking the "living water" of Christ to quench spiritual thirst.</note>, <term>springs of water</term> <note>Latin: fontes aquarum; representing the grace and truth that flow from the Divine.</term>, <term>longing</term> <note>Latin: desiderat; implies a deep, physical necessity, like a parched animal in the desert.</term>

# FROM H. HUGO.

### XLIV.

> *Lord of hosts, how lovely are your tabernacles! My soul languishes and is consumed with the desire to be in the house of the Lord.* <note>original: "Seigneur des armées, que vos tabernacles sont aimables ! Mon ame languit & se consume de désir d'être dans la maison du Seigneur." This is a translation of Psalm 84:1-2.</note>

How desirable is your Tabernacle, O Love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Almighty God, O Lord of virtues!
Beauty as simple as it is adorable,
You hold my senses in suspense:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You carry me out of myself;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I no longer know what I am:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The more extreme my love becomes,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The less I know what I am saying.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Alas! I have lost the power of speech;
Speak for me, You, my sovereign Good:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I come to learn at your school,
Where you instruct me in secret of my Nothingness. <note>The "Nothingness" (Rien) refers to the mystical concept of "le pur néant," where the soul recognizes its total dependence on God.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When I sought you by myself,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I relied upon my own efforts;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But your supreme Wisdom,
In teaching me its marvelous inner workings,
Taught me also how one must love you,
And that I must moderate my transports <note>In this context, "transports" refers to overwhelming emotional or spiritual ecstasies.</note>,
For they are too lowly for such supreme grandeur.

# OF H. HUGO <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest and author of the famous emblem book "Pia Desideria," which used poetry and imagery to describe the soul's journey toward God.</note>

> My heart, my sorrowful heart, shall complain no more,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It loves you now without pretense:
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There is no longer any division:
> I have found the secret of entire union. <note>The "mystical union" refers to the highest state of prayer where the soul feels completely joined with the Divine.</note>
>
> Perfect Being, indivisible, immense,
> Filling all things without occupying space,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He who mourns your absence
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Does not know that you are God. <note>The poet suggests that because God is omnipresent—existing everywhere at once—He cannot truly be "absent"; only our awareness of Him fades.</note>

<summary>A poetic meditation on the soul's total union with God, emphasizing that the Divine presence fills all things and renders feelings of abandonment a misunderstanding of God's nature.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, mystical union, omnipresence, religious poetry, Jesuit spirituality, divine love</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page concludes the series of emblems based on the work of Hermann Hugo (1588–1629), a Jesuit priest whose book "Pia Desideria" became the most popular religious emblem book of the 17th century. The translation maintains the lyrical, devotional tone of the French verse.</meta>

<column-break/>

# C O N C L U S I O N.

Let us conclude that the end of these tender sighs,
      Is the end of all our desires.
What is there to desire besides you, my adorable Master?
Even the heavens without you, sweet Author of my
      being,
Could not satisfy a heart such as mine.
      You are my only Good.
With you, sorrows shall be to my advantage,
Even hell, hell itself, if I were near to you,
      Would be for me a happy lot,
      Its torments would seem sweet to me.
      Heaven and all its delights
      Without you would be as tortures to me.
      To bring this truth into the light,
Let us say that all places, when the heart loves you,
      Will be the same for the heart as long as it is near you:
There is no more torment where your Love reigns.
      Be so tranquil, O my fire <note>The "fire" refers to the burning passion of the soul's divine love.</note>,
      That no spark may escape from it:
Let us have no more sighs, nor fear, nor zeal
      Except for the glory of my GOD.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>This concluding poem summarizes the central theme of the collection: that the soul's only true fulfillment is found in God, whose presence transforms even suffering or hell into joy.</summary>
<keywords>Hermann Hugo, divine love, spiritual desire, Jesuit emblems, devotion, afterlife, mystical union</keywords>

# THE LOVE OF GOD.
<note>original: "L'AMOUR DE DIEU"</note>
<column-break/>
## CONCLUSION
->REGARDING THE<-
# EMBLEMS
## OF OTTO VAN VEEN
<note>Otto van Veen (1556–1629), also known as Otho Vaenius, was a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens. His "Amoris Divini Emblemata" used visual metaphors to teach spiritual truths.</note>
->REGARDING<-
## DIVINE LOVE,
->which represent<-
->the most essential Dispositions<-
->of the Christian interior.<-
<note>The "Christian interior" refers to the inner spiritual life and the state of the soul.</note>

---

> Why do we wait for the end of these established delays,
> Shall we seek some end to our woes?
> We are shown the way without you, sweet Author of our days,
> Toward the places where Peace reigns.
> You alone are my only God,
> With you alone is all my advantage,
> The universe bores me; the universe, if I were near you,
> Would be for me but a mere place of passage.
> The Heaven with all its delights,
> Its brilliance, its order, and its beauties,
> For a heart of which you are the daylight,
> Would be for it, without you, no clarity at all.
> Happy Soul, tranquil, O my God,
> May there be, in the end, no spark,
> And for us not one sigh, of fear, nor of ardor,
> Except for the glory of my GOD.

---

->THE END.<-

<page-type>illustration</page-type>

[**Image Description:** An engraved symbolic illustration contained within a rectangular double-line border. The central subject is a sphere, representing the world or a celestial body, set against a backdrop of radiant light and surrounded by dense, billowing clouds. The sphere is pierced by eight arrows entering from various angles around its circumference, all pointing toward the center. A diagonal axis or rod passes through the center of the sphere, which features a darkened circular core. Rays of light emanate from the space behind the sphere, illuminating the edges of the heavy clouds.]

->IT PIERCES THROUGH AND SUSTAINS<-
<note>original Latin: "PERFIGIT ET SVSTINET." This motto suggests a paradox: the same force (represented by the arrows) that penetrates the world also provides its underlying support or stability.</note>

**Technical Terms:**

*   <term>Perfigit</term>: To pierce through or transfix. In this context, it refers to the arrows or rays entering the globe.
*   <term>Sustinet</term>: To uphold, support, or keep from falling. This implies that the central axis or the forces acting upon the sphere are what keep the universe in its proper order.
*   <term>Emblem</term>: A traditional teaching tool consisting of a mysterious image and a motto, intended to be meditated upon to reveal a deeper truth.
*   <term>Sphere</term>: A common symbol for the created universe or the human soul.
*   <term>Arrows</term>: Often used in this period to represent divine rays, influence, or the mathematical vectors of light and force.

<summary>This page features a symbolic emblem of a sphere pierced by arrows, illustrating the concept of a force that simultaneously penetrates and supports the world. It bridges the gap between spiritual allegory and the era's emerging interest in the physical laws of light and motion.</summary>
<keywords>emblem, sphere, arrows, light, physics, divine influence, stability, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>The catchword "O Ver-" from the previous page likely introduces the title or the first line of this poem, which addresses "O Verbe" (O Word) or "O Veritable Love." This page continues the theological reflection on the omnipresence of Divine Love.</meta>

## Love penetrates & sustains the Universe.

Love, you who penetrate the Universe with your arrows,
Who by that same effect sustain your own handiwork,
Everything reveals you, O great God, everything bears
witness to you;
Every object brings you forth in a hundred different
ways.
(a) Certainly man here below has no right to
complain
That you hide yourself too much from his weak gaze;
You have known how to paint yourself everywhere so vividly
That the eye which chooses to open sees you on every side.
But the most beautiful mark of your grandeur,
And one which does not depend at all on the report of our eyes,
Is that when one seeks you with a faithful heart,
One finds you within oneself even better than in any other place.

---

*(a) These Verses are taken from Mr. de Brébeuf <note>Guillaume de Brébeuf (1617–1661), a French poet known for his sacred works and his translation of Lucan.</note> with a small amount of change.*

<summary>A poetic meditation on the omnipresence of Divine Love, arguing that God is visible throughout the natural world but most intimately discovered within the human heart.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, omnipresence, Guillaume de Brébeuf, spiritual interiority, creation, witness of nature</keywords>

Divine Child, who deserves
That all the world adore you,
Must it be that after so much kindness
No one here implores you?

We live in eternal forgetfulness
Of your favors and of yourself;
I suffer to see that today
Almost no one loves you.

People wish to be seen as generous
While practicing the darkest ingratitude:
Child, the delight of the heavens,
How distressing it is to me, how harsh it is
To be unable to find a heart
That is pierced by your flames <note>The "flames" refer to the "fire of divine love," a common mystical image representing intense devotion.</note>,
And of which you are the possessor,
Penetrating the depths of our souls.

Child so charming and so sweet,
Ah, bring everything under your empire! <note>original: "empire" - here meaning his spiritual reign or sovereign rule over human hearts.</note>
Since my heart is entirely yours,
Grant it what it desires.

<gloss>Divine Child, devotion, prayer, ingratitude, empire, souls</gloss>

The waters of Siloam <note>original: "Siloë"; a reference to the Pool of Siloam in Jerusalem, often used in literature to symbolize divine grace or healing</note>, so calm and tranquil,
->Through a frightful misfortune,<-
Froze one day, and its useful washing-pools

# EMBLEM I.

->*We must love God above all things.*<-

No, the heart cannot comprehend
The blessings that you prepare for it;
The eye cannot see, nor the ear hear
That with which you will reward
The loving Soul who adores you:
But, O Beauty that remains unknown <note>The author refers to the "Cloud of Unknowing" or the hidden nature of God that transcends human senses.</note>,
The heart, by not understanding,
Finds that its extreme Love,
Drawn by such adorable charms,
Invites it to step outside of itself <note>A reference to "ecstasy," from the Greek *ekstasis*, meaning to stand outside oneself in spiritual rapture.</note>.

It knows that you are a Good
To which, Lord, all others yield,
Since as soon as one possesses you
The heart asks for nothing more.

Finally enlightened by faith
It feels everything within itself fail,
Luminous in its poverty <note>The concept of "spiritual poverty" or "indigence" where the soul is emptied of worldly things to be filled with light.</note>,
By losing all intellectual understanding
It understands that a Sovereign Good,
Enclosing all in itself through extreme happiness,
Must make all things happy through itself.
It says everything by saying nothing.

<vocab>Emblem, God, Love, Sovereign Good, Faith, Loving Soul</vocab>

<summary>The first emblem of the collection explores the mystical paradox of divine love, where the soul finds true understanding and fulfillment by moving beyond human senses and intellectual reason.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, mysticism, spiritual ecstasy, Sovereign Good, faith, transcendence</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

## II.

->*We must begin.*<-

> Buried in misery,
> Overwhelmed by a thousand sins
> To which all my senses are bound,
> I was on the verge of perishing: My charitable Father,
> Touched by so many diverse evils,
> Extends a helping hand to me,
> Opens my eyes, breaks my chains.
> To make a happy man out of a miserable man
> He asks for nothing but my consent:
> But a false error, which flatters and enchants me,
> Makes me prefer my torment
> To His goodness, so tender and so touching.
> Ah, how I hate this heart, how ungrateful I find it!
> Lord, show Your power,
> Tear away from this villain <note>The "villain" or "scoundrel" refers to the speaker's own sinful nature or the "old self."</note>
> Even the liberty to offer resistance.

<sig>H</sig>
<meta>catchword: III. The A</meta>

<keywords>spiritual struggle, divine grace, repentance, free will, internal conflict, mercy</keywords>

# OF O. VANIUS.

### III.

### Adoption comes from Love.

Love presents me to his Father,
The Father receives me for the sake of his Son;
The Son treats me as a brother,
He shares with me the wealth that he has won.
Happy **ADOPTION**, which grants the inheritance
To the vile slave of the Demon,
And gives him the right to the portion
Of the sole Heir of holy Zion! <note>Zion refers to the celestial city or the kingdom of God.</note>

The **FATHER** gives a Son there to save a slave,
The **SON** gives himself and returns the ransomed slave,
The **HOLY SPIRIT** associates with this divine conclave <note>The term "conclave" here suggests a private or sacred assembly of the Trinity.</note>
The servant adopted by **THE FATHER**.

O Mystery of Love, who can understand you,
That the **ALL** for the **NOTHING** deigns to descend to earth,
To make himself Man so that man might be God! <note>A reference to the "divine exchange" or theosis, a common theme in mystical theology.</note>
Be still, my Reason, remain in silence,
This is the time and the place
To let nothing speak but Gratitude.

<sig>H 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: IV. L'A-</meta>

<vocab>Adoption, Love, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Zion, Mystery, Gratitude</vocab>

<summary>This page features a poetic meditation on the theological concept of adoption, explaining how divine love transforms a "vile slave" into an heir of heaven through the cooperation of the Trinity. It concludes with a call for human reason to fall silent in the face of such a mystery, leaving room only for gratitude.</summary>
<keywords>Adoption, Divine Love, Trinity, Salvation, Mysticism, Zion, Otto Vaenius, Spiritual Inheritance</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, following the previous meditations on the nature of divine affection. The focus here shifts to the "uprightness" or "rectitude" of the soul's orientation toward God.</meta>

# EMBLEMS

### IV.

->Love is upright.<-

Love probes the human heart,
He desires a pure will,
And recognizes by its uprightness
Whether the love one bears him is a sovereign Love <note>Sovereign Love refers to a love that is supreme and holds total authority over the heart.</note>.
If it leans toward the earth even slightly,
If it distances itself from him even a little,
Or seeks a support within itself,
It cannot pass for a sincere Lover.
When the heart loves purely,
It tends incessantly toward the divine Object:
The rest appears to it like the glitter of glass,
As fragile as it is deceptive.
It is true that Love alone is the weight of the heart; <note>A reference to St. Augustine’s famous phrase, "My love is my weight" (Amor meus, pondus meum), meaning that love is the gravity that pulls the soul toward its destination.</note>
As the Love is, so is the choice.
Give, give to my heart, Great God, this rectitude <term>rectitude: moral uprightness or a straight spiritual path</term>;
It will then be without bias and without anxiety;
Envisioning only your goodness,
Its only inclination will be your truth.

<meta>catchword: V. The L</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the necessity of a "straight" or upright heart that seeks God alone, warning that any self-interest or earthly attachment renders love insincere. It concludes with a prayer for divine rectitude to eliminate anxiety and bias.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, purity of heart, rectitude, spiritual gravity, sincerity, devotion</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### IV.

->Upright Love<- 
<note>original: "Amor rectus"</note>

Love probes the human heart,
He desires a pure will,
And recognizes by its uprightness <note>In the accompanying illustration, this rectitude is symbolized by a plumb line held by a winged cherub.</note>
Whether the love one bears him is a sovereign Love <note>Sovereign Love refers to a love that is supreme and holds total authority over the heart.</note>.
If it leans toward the earth even slightly,
If it distances itself from him even a little,
Or seeks a support within itself,
It cannot pass for a sincere Lover.
When the heart loves purely,
It tends incessantly toward the divine Object:
The rest appears to it like the glitter of glass,
As fragile as it is deceptive.
It is true that Love alone is the weight of the heart; <note>A reference to St. Augustine’s famous phrase, "My love is my weight" (original: "Amor meus, pondus meum"), meaning that love is the spiritual gravity that pulls the soul toward its true home.</note>
As the Love is, so is the choice.
Give, give to my heart, Great God, this rectitude <term>rectitude: moral uprightness or a straight spiritual path, often compared to a straight line in geometry</term>;
It will then be without bias and without anxiety;
Envisioning only your goodness,
Its only inclination will be your truth.

<meta>catchword: V. The L</meta>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of a plumb line to explore the necessity of a "straight" or upright heart that seeks God alone, warning that any self-interest or earthly attachment renders love insincere. It concludes with a prayer for divine rectitude to eliminate anxiety and bias.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, purity of heart, rectitude, spiritual gravity, sincerity, devotion, St. Augustine</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems. The previous page (IV) concluded with a prayer for "rectitude," and the catchword "The L" transitions into this fifth emblem, which focuses on the enduring nature of that divine connection.</meta>

# EMBLEMS

### V.

->The Love Eternal<- 
<note>original Latin: "Amor aeternus"</note>

In this scene, we witness a sacred pledge of constancy. Within a circle formed by an **Ouroboros** <term>Ouroboros: an ancient symbol of a serpent biting its own tail, representing eternity, the infinite, and the cyclical nature of the universe</term> lying upon the earth, two winged **putti** <term>putti: winged infant figures common in Renaissance and Baroque art, often representing different forms of Love</term> meet in a solemn handshake. 

The figure on the left represents Divine Love, distinguished by a radiant halo of light surrounding his head. He reaches out to the second figure, who is dressed in a simple tunic, representing the human soul or a faithful companion. Their handshake—a traditional gesture of "fides" or solemn trust—signifies a covenant that cannot be broken. 

The background landscape, featuring rolling hills and fortified towers, represents the temporal world. While the birds fly through a cloudy sky and the seasons of the world may change, the figures remain centered within the serpent’s ring, indicating that their affection is protected from the ravages of time and remains forever within the realm of the infinite.

<summary>This emblem illustrates the concept of eternal love using the symbol of the Ouroboros and a handshake between Divine Love and the soul. It emphasizes that a true spiritual union is a lasting covenant that transcends the passage of time and the physical world.</summary>

<keywords>Eternal Love, Ouroboros, Divine Love, covenant, eternity, handshake, symbolism, constancy</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic reflections based on the emblems of Otto Vaenius (O. Vanius), transitioning from previous themes of earthly vanity to the constancy of Divine Love.</meta>

### V.

# *Love is Eternal.*

How happy one is in loving you,
->Since one loves eternally.<-
Everything that is not you, which one sees in the world,
->Is more inconstant than the waves.<-

The pleasures here below have only a deceptive paint <note>The original "fard" refers to cosmetic makeup, implying a superficial or false beauty.</note>,
Honors and wealth pass away like the wind:
You remain always, you are immutable,
Everything that you give is charming and lasting:
And when a young heart gives itself to your Love
You repay its sighs with a happy return.

->This Love is free from weakness and from fear,<-
->It is sincere, it is without feigning:<-
When you inflame <note>original: "enflamez"; here meaning to set the heart on fire with divine passion</note>, you feel the fires;
When you bind my heart, I hold you in my
->knots.<-

This reciprocal Love is constant and faithful,
->Its chain is eternal:<-
It is great, it is holy, it is victorious,
And furthermore it is sure of being always happy.

<sig>H 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: VI. L'A-</meta>

<summary>A devotional poem asserting that while worldly pleasures and honors are fleeting and inconstant, Divine Love is immutable, eternal, and provides a reciprocal, lasting happiness.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, eternity, constancy, Otto Vaenius, spiritual devotion, vanity of the world</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, following the previous meditations on the soul's relationship with the Divine.</meta>

<header>EMBLEMS</header>

## VI.

### *The Love of God is the Sun of the Soul.*

May your rays, dear Spouse of my heart,
Enlighten and penetrate my soul:
Be my only conqueror,
May I burn forever with your sweet flame!
How my heart is charmed by your divine attractions!
How happy I find it to be under your empire!
It is a delicious martyrdom <note>The author uses "martyrdom" here in the mystical sense—a joyful suffering or "dying to oneself" caused by divine love.</note>
To be wounded by your arrows.

The more you wound, the more one loves you;
I even adore the rigor
Which, by taking me away from myself,
Leaves me nothing sweet or flattering <note>original: "flateur." In this context, it refers to worldly vanities or self-centered pleasures that distract from God.</note>.

No more **ME**! nothing but **YOU**! let every object
fade away!
I feel myself lifted by a noble audacity:
All that is not you, is unworthy of me.
In you alone my hope is founded;
Content to have you as my King,
With contempt I look upon all the rest of the world.

<image-desc>A faint, decorative tailpiece engraving at the bottom of the text depicting a landscape with trees and foliage.</image-desc>

<sig>H</sig>
<meta>VII. The L</meta>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of the sun and arrows of love to describe the soul's total surrender to God, emphasizing the joy found in spiritual "martyrdom" and the rejection of the self.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Mysticism, Spiritual Marriage, Self-Abnegation, Sun Metaphor, Devotional Poetry</keywords>

# VII.

## *Love sees itself crowned with a great reward.*

<note>This poem is likely a commentary on an accompanying illustration by Otto van Veen (O. Vænius), a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens known for his emblem books.</note>

I told you so, fortunate Lover,
->What your happiness would one day be:<-
->What an admirable destiny!<-
God gives Himself to the one who gives Him their heart:
You give Him yours, and He gives Himself;
He is your Creator, and His extreme Love
->Makes Him your debtor.<-
->Oh, what an admirable reward!<-
If one is more than repaid by a single day in His presence,
->What is it to be eternally<-
The Spouse of Him whom the Angels revere,
->In whom all men place their hope,<-
And found their happiness and their contentment?

<summary>This page features a devotional poem reflecting on the spiritual rewards of divine love, framing the relationship between the soul and God as a reciprocal exchange that ends in eternal union.</summary>
<keywords>Otto van Veen, Divine Love, spiritual marriage, salvation, devotion, emblems, 17th-century poetry</keywords>

<center>E M B L E M S</center>

## VIII.

### *Love Instructs.*

Teach me, my Divine Master,
&nbsp;&nbsp;To do your will well:
Eternally I wish to be
Docile to the laws that your goodness prescribes.
This incomparable doctrine
Has nothing but the sacred, nothing but the divine;
May my heart, like a tablet <note>The author refers to the "table of the heart," a common biblical metaphor for the soul's receptivity to God's word.</note>
Be engraved with it by your divine hand!
This law teaches us to leave everything behind,
To follow its Legislator.
The sacred precepts that Love proposes to us
Are solid, are sweet, and have nothing of the flatterer.
&nbsp;&nbsp;He who follows them finds life therein,
&nbsp;&nbsp;He who flees them encounters death:
He who follows them out of Love, finds that his fate
&nbsp;&nbsp;Becomes worthy of envy;
&nbsp;&nbsp;Since this entirely divine Master
Gives as a prize a happiness that never ends.

<insert>IX. L'A-</insert> <note>This catchword at the bottom of the page indicates the start of the next emblem, "L'Amour..." (Love...).</note>

<term>Emblems</term> <note>A genre of literature combining symbolic pictures with poetic moral lessons.</note>, <term>Love</term> <note>In this mystical context, "Love" refers to Divine Love or Christ.</note>, <term>Divine Master</term>, <term>will</term>, <term>doctrine</term>, <term>heart</term>, <term>Legislator</term>, <term>precepts</term>, <term>happiness</term>

<summary>This emblem depicts the soul as a student of Divine Love, asking to have God's laws engraved upon the heart to achieve eternal happiness. It emphasizes that following divine precepts leads to life, while rejecting them leads to spiritual death.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual instruction, obedience, the heart as a tablet, eternal life, mystical theology</keywords>

## IX.

<note>The central illustration and the cursive text at the bottom are extremely faint and mirrored, indicating they are bleed-through or an offset—a "ghost image" produced when ink from a facing page or the reverse side was pressed against this leaf over time.</note>

# FROM O. VAENIUS.

### IX.

->*Love is a most dear & precious treasure.*<-

Where one places his treasure, there he also places his heart:
If your treasure is God, God alone is your wealth;
It is there that one tastes a guaranteed happiness,
->Possessing true wisdom.<-

The world has deceptive charms <note>original: "apas" (appas), meaning attractions or lures</note>,
Which tickle the spirit, but leave it entirely empty:
Divine Love has favors,
Whose sweetness is charming & solid.

The world promises us, & gives us nothing:
JESUS gives us all things;
One finds in him the true good,
The world has more thorns than roses.
You shall be, O my God, my precious treasure,
To all others but you I wish to close my eyes.

<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: X. L'A-</meta>

<summary>A spiritual poem contrasting the empty, deceptive attractions of the material world with the lasting sweetness and security found in Divine Love and Jesus.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual treasure, vanity of the world, wisdom, Jesus, Otto Vaenius, emblems</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

->X.<-

->Love is pure.<-

Whoever knows the purity of divine Love
Avoids sin, flees the slightest stain,
And seeks nothing but the truth;
Everything that is not God appears to him as a deception.
Look into this mirror at pure charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to *Caritas*, the highest form of selfless, divine love.</note>,
It is a faithful portrait of it:
The smallest spot tarnishes its beauty.

A single breath prevents the image
From being seen there perfectly:
When one makes a holy use of pure Love,
One surely sees all objects exactly as they are.

One sees nothing in God that is not God himself;
One ceases to see oneself, through an extreme happiness:
Everything disappears, only God remains,
God everywhere, God as everything, in every place.

Whoever sees Him always in this way
Has no eyes left but those of faith:
His Love is entirely pure, his Hope is strong;
Then his Charity carries him
Into the bosom of his King.

<sig>XI. Da</sig>
<vocab>Divine Love, purity, charity, mirror, God, faith, Hope</vocab>
