<keywords>divine love, purity, mysticism, mirror metaphor, charity, faith, spiritual transformation</keywords>

An engraving within a double-line rectangular frame. In the center, two winged figures stand in a landscape. ->*In unity, perfection.*<- <note>original Latin: "In unitate perfectio."</note>

<vocab>unity, perfection, Cupid, Divine Love</vocab>

# EMBLEMS

->XI.<-

->Perfection is found in the One.<-

<note>The Roman numeral "I" represents the Divine One—the source and end of all things. In the mystical tradition, the "One" is God, and "Unity" is the state where the soul is no longer divided by worldly desires but is perfectly joined to Him.</note>

The figures of the Soul and Divine Love lift the symbol of Unity together, crowning it with a laurel wreath to signify the victory of reaching this high spiritual state. They stand upon a stone marked with many numbers <note>the numbers 3, 4, 7, 8, 9, 10, and 11</note>, which represent the multiplicity and complexity of the created world. To reach perfection, the soul must rise above these "many" things—various disciplines, laws, and worldly distractions—to rest in the simplicity of the "One."

Just as the chapel in the background points its spire toward heaven, the heart must point toward a single goal. When the soul is no longer scattered among many interests, it finds its true completion. <note>This reflects a central theme in 17th-century French spirituality: the "Unitive Way," where the soul loses its own fragmented will to find a perfect, simple life in God.</note>

<summary>This emblem depicts the Soul and Divine Love exalting the number 'One,' symbolizing that spiritual perfection is achieved by moving beyond the multiplicity of the world into a state of total unity with God.</summary>
<keywords>unity, perfection, Divine Love, the Soul, sacred numbers, mystical union, spiritual victory</keywords>

# XI.

->*In Unity is found perfection.*<-

Sacred Love <note>original: "L’Amour ſacré"</note> suffers no division,
->It is simple; it is Truth;<-
->It alone has the advantage<-
Of reducing everything to unity.

->In God all things are one,<-
There is nothing outside of him but division,
->But troubles and misfortunes;<-
Calm and happiness are found only in Union.

->JESUS asked this for his own from his Father;<- <note>A reference to the "High Priestly Prayer" in John 17:21, "that they all may be one."</note>
It is this divine calm that he gives to his friends.
Admirable Unity, the ONE THING NECESSARY! <note>original: "l’UNIQUE NÉCESSAIRE"; a reference to Luke 10:42 regarding Mary and Martha.</note>
It is you who makes all hearts firm in God;
->It is you who makes sorrows sweet,<-
->Who makes the harshest labors light:<-
->You break the chains of your captives,<-
And you make them find pleasure even in their ills.

# E M B L E M S

## XII.

->*Love has its divine battles.*<-

What is this that appears before my eyes?
Is it a matter of the earth, or a matter of the heavens?
Who shall carry away the victory?
Shall the victor possess the glory?
I know not what to think of this strange combat;
Who is the Captain, and who is the soldier?
If I could but enter into this celebrated duel,
I would find my happiness in my captivity.
Has this divine Conqueror <note>The "Conqueror" here refers to Divine Love or Christ, a common trope in 17th-century devotional poetry.</note> not deserved
That His glory be celebrated in every place?
But if I remain the victor,
He becomes my captive and I win His heart;
By losing against Him, I gain the victory:
Whether victor or vanquished, He has all the glory.

<meta>catchword: XIII. L'A</meta>

<summary>Emblem XII explores the paradoxical "divine combat" between the soul and God, where surrendering to Divine Love results in a spiritual victory for both parties.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual warfare, paradox of faith, captivity, victory, glory, sacred poetry</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

## XIII.

->Let there be Reciprocity in Love<-
<note>original Latin: "Sit in Amore reciprocatio."</note>

->*May there be a mutual return in Love.*<-

<note>This emblem illustrates a central theme in 17th-century devotional art: the "Amoris reciprocitas" or the mutual exchange of love between God and the human soul. The engraving depicts two winged <term>putti</term> (child-like figures of Love). The figure on the left, distinguished by a radiant halo, represents Divine Love. He has already been "wounded" by an arrow, symbolizing God’s initiative in loving humanity. The figure on the right represents the Human Soul, who returns this affection by aiming his own bow at the Divine. The background landscape with its steeples and hillside buildings suggests that this spiritual exchange takes place within the context of the world, yet is directed toward the heavens.</note>

<summary>Emblem XIII depicts the concept of spiritual reciprocity, showing Divine Love and the Human Soul exchanging arrows to represent a mutual, reflected affection between the creator and the believer.</summary>

<keywords>reciprocity, Divine Love, Cupid, Soul, arrows, spiritual exchange, sacred emblems, mutual love</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
### XIII.

->*Love loves reciprocity.*<-

What do I see? Love, who wounds his Beloved,
And who also lets himself be wounded by her in turn!
With her heart pierced by arrows and a smiling face,
She appears content,
And takes up new arrows to aim back at Love.

> If these blows are mortal,
> How lovely death is!
> And if they are not so,
> How desirable it would be
> To receive blows
> So charming and so sweet!

Love, strike me often with such wounds;
The blows that come from your hand,
Despite my harshest pains,
Are a sovereign balm <note>A "sovereign balm" refers to a supreme or ultimate healing ointment, implying that the pain of love is its own cure.</note> for my heart.

<vocab>Love, Beloved, heart, arrows <note>original: "traits" - often used in Baroque poetry to refer to Cupid's darts</note>, sovereign balm, reciprocal</vocab>

<summary>This page features an emblem and poem describing the mutual exchange of affection between Love (Cupid) and a lover, suggesting that the "wounds" of love are pleasurable when they are returned. It emphasizes the theme of reciprocity as a healing force even when it causes emotional pain.</summary>
<keywords>reciprocity, Cupid, arrows of love, healing wounds, Otto van Veen, Baroque emblems</keywords>

### XIV.

->*Virtue is but the mark of Love.*<-

## Virtue is the Mark of Love
<note>original Latin: "Virtus Character Amoris"</note>

# EMBLEMS

### XIV.

->*Virtue is but the mark of Love.*<-

Of all the virtues, Love is the source;
->It brings them to life within our hearts<-
Just as the Sun brings forth a thousand flowers
->In its brilliant course.<-

The fire of holy Love, by its sweet warmth,
Produces in us fortitude and prudence,
->Justice and temperance,<- <note>These four—Strength, Wisdom, Justice, and Moderation—are known as the "Cardinal Virtues," the pillars of a moral life.</note>
->Chastity and humble gentleness:<-

Charity, which crowns the virtues,
->Is also their foundation:<- <note>In this context, "Charity" refers to "Caritas" or Divine Love, considered the highest of the three theological virtues.</note>
If you wish to possess them, love sincerely,
->Since it is LOVE who bestows them.<-

<term>Divine Love</term> (Amour): The selfless, spiritual love for the Divine and one's neighbor that motivates all good deeds.
<term>Virtue</term> (Vertu): A moral excellence or a quality that is deemed to be morally good.
<term>Fortitude</term> (Force): The mental and emotional strength in facing difficulty, adversity, or danger.
<term>Prudence</term> (Prudence): The ability to govern and discipline oneself by the use of reason and wisdom.
<term>Justice</term> (Justice): The virtue of fairness and the maintenance of what is right.
<term>Temperance</term> (Temperance): Moderation in action, thought, or feeling; self-restraint.
<term>Scales</term>: A traditional symbol of Justice and balance, held by the figure on the right in the illustration.
<term>Bow</term>: A traditional attribute of Cupid, here representing the "arrows" of divine love that strike the heart.

<summary>Emblem XIV presents Divine Love (Charity) as the "Sun" of the soul, arguing that all other moral virtues like justice and prudence are merely reflections or products of this central, holy fire. The accompanying illustration uses two figures—one representing Divine Love and the other moral balance—to show how spiritual devotion leads to an ordered, virtuous life.</summary>
<keywords>Emblem XIV, Divine Love, Charity, Cardinal Virtues, moral philosophy, sun metaphor, spiritual growth</keywords>

# FROM O. VAENIUS. <note>Otto Vaenius (1556–1629) was a Flemish painter and scholar, famously the teacher of Peter Paul Rubens. This text is a French poetic adaptation of his Latin emblems on Divine Love.</note>

### XV.

->*It is the unanimous union of two wills.*<-

How much strength and power we would possess
If, far from dividing our desires without success,
    A sincere obedience
    Made up our innocent pleasures!

    When one lives in dependence
    Upon the supreme will,
    One finds prompt assistance
    In the care taken by His Goodness.

The heaviest burden becomes a light load
    Assured of such help;
        Far from dragging out one's days
        In sad misery,
One finds, even in the midst of torments,
    Sweet contentment.

    Perfect Love <note>In this context, "Amour" (Love) refers to the soul's divine love for God, often personified in emblem books as a child or Cupid-like figure representing the Soul.</note> does not count as pain
        That which it does for its King;
    And His sovereign will
        Is at all times its law:
    Nothing fatigues or constrains it,
Everything yields to this Love, and everything yields to its Faith.

<meta>catchword: XVI. It is</meta>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XV from Vaenius, focusing on the theme of "Unanimous Will," where the human soul finds strength and joy by aligning its desires entirely with the divine will of God.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, Divine Love, obedience, sovereign will, faith, spiritual strength, contentment in suffering</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XVI.

->*It is upward that he gazes.*<-

> Perfect **Love**, just like this flower <note>The "flower of the Sun" or Heliotrope (sunflower), a common seventeenth-century symbol for the soul following God.</note>
> Turns incessantly toward the supreme Beauty:
> Without ever looking at itself,
> It sees only **GOD ALONE** who possesses its heart.
> 
> This flower of the Sun follows the course without ceasing,
> In the same way this docile soul
> Always follows the divine will;
> It is its strength and its refuge:
> 
> One never sees it toward any other side
> Turn or fix its sight:
> This soul is every day stretched <note>The original French "tendue" implies a spiritual tension or yearning, similar to the "stretching out" described by Saint Augustine.</note>
> Toward the celestial truth.
> 
> **GOD ALONE** is its pleasure, **GOD ALONE** is its
> wealth,
> All that is not Him could not touch it:
> I see well that without searching too much
> It has found the solid Wisdom.

<sig>XVII. R</sig>

<vocab>Love, supreme Beauty, God Alone, flower of the Sun, docile soul, divine will, celestial truth, solid Wisdom</vocab>

# XVII.

->*It increases beyond measure.*<-

When the heart, like a pure mirror <note>The original French uses "glace," which can mean ice or a mirror; given the context of reflecting the sun, "mirror" is the intended meaning here.</note>,
Receives the impression of this divine Sun,
Its fire grows beyond measure;
And this unparalleled fire
Is full of a charming sweetness
Which burns in peace without causing pain:
The soul is cheerful and content
Even in the midst of its greatest fervor.

Divine Love, oh how your sweet flame
Consumes my soul in this way!
Do not spare my heart at all:
Reduce it entirely to ashes,
For is there anything more tender
Than your holy rigor? <note>The "holy rigor" refers to the transformative, sometimes painful process of spiritual purification.</note>

You come to cleanse me of what is contrary to you,
You beautify me, you overwhelm me with peace,
You put me in a state to be able from now on
To please you perfectly:
O infinite happiness of sovereign Love!
Make it so that within my heart your fires grow without end.

<sig>K</sig>
<meta>catchword: XVIII. Pre-</meta>

<summary>This page features Emblem XVII from a collection inspired by Otto Vaenius, using the metaphor of a mirror reflecting the sun to describe how divine love intensifies within the soul. It portrays spiritual purification as a "sweet flame" that brings peace and beauty to the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual purification, Otto Vaenius, soul's transformation, sacred fire, emblems</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### X V I I I.

->*Preferable to the love of both father and mother.*<-

He who does not leave for me
That which he holds most dear, even Father and Mother,
Can never please me,
Nor give me proof of his faith;
But he who for my Love
Abandons all things,
Deserves the crown
Of the eternal dwelling.

If I still cling to the earth,
To friends, goods, and Parents—alas, this triple knot
Overwhelms me with misery
And renders me unworthy of God.
But if I leave all things
To follow my JESUS and die upon the Cross,
Let Him be both the end and the cause
Of this so righteous a choice;
He crowns His own gifts by crowning our merits <note>A reference to the Augustinian doctrine that when God rewards our merits, He is actually crowning His own work within us.</note>
With a happiness so perfect that it has no limits.

<meta>catchword: XIX. THE</meta>

<summary>Emblem XVIII emphasizes the necessity of prioritizing divine love over earthly family ties and material possessions to achieve eternal life. It reflects the biblical mandate to leave all behind to follow Christ and find perfection in His grace.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, renunciation, martyrdom, eternal life, spiritual crown, devotion, Jesus Christ</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### X V I I I.

<page-num>XVIII.</page-num>

[Illustration: A copperplate engraving showing two winged figures, or putti, walking hand-in-hand through a pastoral landscape. The figure on the right, representing Divine Love, has a radiant halo and points toward a small crucifix mounted on a stone pedestal. ->*More powerful than devotion to parents.*<- <note>original: "Pietate in parentes potior"</note>

He who does not leave for my sake
That which he holds most dear, even Father and Mother,
Can never truly please me,
Nor give me proof of his faith;
But he who for love of me
Abandons all things,
Deserves the crown
Of the eternal dwelling.

If I still cling to the earth,
To friends, goods, and Parents—alas, this triple knot <note>In spiritual literature, the "triple knot" often refers to the worldly attachments that bind the soul: the desire for pleasure, the desire for possessions, and the pride of life.</note>
Overwhelms me with misery
And renders me unworthy of God.
But if I leave all things
To follow my JESUS and die upon the Cross,
Let Him be both the end and the cause
Of this so righteous a choice;
He crowns His own gifts by crowning our merits <note>A reference to the Augustinian doctrine that when God rewards our merits, He is actually crowning His own work within us.</note>
With a happiness so perfect that it has no limits.

<vocab>Devotion, parents, preferable, J. Smit, storks, crucifix, winged figures (putti), filial piety</vocab>

<meta>catchword: XIX. THE</meta>

<summary>Emblem XVIII illustrates the biblical command to prioritize divine love over family ties, using the stork as a symbol of earthly filial piety that must be surpassed by devotion to Christ. The text argues that true spiritual merit is achieved only when earthly "knots" are untied in favor of the Cross.</summary>

# FROM O. VÆNIUS.

### XIX.

->*Love is the bond of perfection.*<-
<note>original: "L' Amour est le lien de la perfection." This is a reference to Colossians 3:14.</note>

<image-desc>The text is printed over a faint copperplate engraving of a landscape. In the background on the right, a small round tower with a conical roof and a flag sits atop a hill, surrounded by sparse trees under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

How charming these knots are, and how pre-
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;cious!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How worthy of envy!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through them the soul sees itself united
To the Sovereign Lord of the earth and the heavens.

Sacred knot, by which Love unites with his Beloved,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through an excess of charity!
<note>In this context, "charity" refers to 'caritas' or divine, selfless love.</note>
It makes her, even now in time, a participant
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the happiness of Eternity.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How beautiful this chain is
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since it is eternal!
Let us then allow ourselves to be bound by these charming ties,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since they are immutable,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since they are entirely divine.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They are entirely desirable,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;These sacred and sweet knots
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of my Divine Spouse.
<note>The "Divine Spouse" is a common mystical metaphor for Christ.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O, how they are preferable
To all the goods and charms the world possesses!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How lovely I find them!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Rigorous death
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Does not dissolve, does not break
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;These knots, though they be so tender,
Since the sacred fire still burns beneath our ashes.

<sig>K 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: XX. It</meta>

<summary>This emblem verse celebrates the spiritual "knots" or bonds of divine love that unite the soul with God, asserting that this sacred connection transcends earthly pleasures and even survives death. It utilizes the "Divine Spouse" imagery common in 17th-century mystical literature.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, mystical union, Otto van Veen, eternity, sacred marriage, spiritual bonds, charity</keywords>

<header>EMBLEMS</header>

## XX.

### *He is the conqueror of nature.*

I do not fear nature
Whatever pain I endure,
Since sacred Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) as a personification of the Divine or Christ.</note> wishes to be my support:
Surely with him
I carry away the victory;
But if he alone holds all the glory,
He crowns my faith,
And shares with me
The fruit of his conquests;
We hold a thousand celebrations,
And this charming victor,
As the prize for so many blessings, wants only our heart.
Take it, dear Love, oh take it yourself,
Command that it love you.
What! is a command necessary
To love this charming Victor?
Ah, how extreme the misfortune is
To not love you at all! Alas!
Can one truly live, and not be
Wholly transported out of oneself,
Seeing that such a charming Victor
Gives us the express command to love him?

<meta>catchword: XXI.</meta>

<summary>This emblem describes the soul's victory over the physical world through the support of Divine Love, concluding that loving God is a joyful necessity rather than a burdensome command.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, victory over nature, spiritual conquest, the heart, sacred devotion</keywords>

The imagery and text shift from internal contemplation to an active, albeit painful, spiritual conquest.</meta>

# XXI.

->*He keeps us from harm.*<-

No, no, I no longer fear the winds nor the storm,
Protected by Divine Love <note>The author uses "Amour divin" to personify God's affection as a protective guardian.</note>
I feel a new courage within me:
Ah, what could I fear? He holds me by the hand.
He serves as my rampart: I live in safety
Surrounded by powerful enemies;
With such assistance
In the midst of danger, I feel my senses grow calm.
I hear the waves roaring; I see the lightning strike;
I see everything at my side reduced to dust:
What have I to fear for myself?
I remain at rest under the shadow of his wing,
His Love fills me with both strength and zeal.
For so much care, he asks only for my faith;
To him I abandon myself:
Without thinking further of myself, I give this entire **S E L F** to him.

<sig>K 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: XXII. He</meta>

<summary>This page features a devotional poem illustrating how Divine Love acts as a shield against life's metaphorical and literal storms, emphasizing total surrender to God. It reflects the 17th-century emblem book tradition where art and poetry combine to provide moral and spiritual instruction.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, protection, providence, faith, spiritual courage, Otto Vaenius, emblem book</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

## XXII.

<image-desc>A faint, ghosted background engraving depicts a pastoral scene. In the foreground, a figure appears to be stooped over, sowing seeds in a field. Other figures and a soft landscape with clouds are visible in the background, consistent with the theme of the poem.</image-desc>

->*He sows and makes the Spirit fruitful.*<-
<note>The original French "second" here carries the archaic meaning of "fecund" or "fruitful."</note>

O Pure & holy Charity,
You cast the good seed
Which, through divine hope,
Bears its fruit even into Eternity!

Happy is he who sows in tears!
How precious are his labors!
Since, in exchange for feeble alarms,
He shall see himself crowned in the heavens.

Here one sows with sorrow;
There one reaps in joy
A hundredfold for one's labor;
And Divine Love grants
To all those who become His own
A thousand honors, a thousand blessings;
For light pains,
For a little misery,
An assured happiness.
How happy then is the heart
That pure Love inflames!
This noble & beautiful soul,
At all times, in every place,
Lives no longer but in its God.

O how great is the abundance
That the seed of heaven
Produces within its breast!
Pure & divine Love
Waters it and makes it grow;
Even already in this mortal abode
One sees appearing everywhere
The fruits of holy Love.

<vocab>Charity, seed, hope, Eternity, pure Love, God, fruits</vocab>

<keywords>Charity, spiritual growth, divine love, sowing and reaping, eternity, mysticism, suffering and reward</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

## XXIII.

<image-desc>A rectangular copperplate engraving. On the right, a winged, haloed child (representing Divine Love or an angel) holds a bow in one hand and points toward heaven with the other. In the center, another winged child stands with hands folded across his chest, looking toward a group of four geese or swans on the ground. The background shows a hilly landscape with buildings under a sky where sunbeams break through thick clouds. The signature "I. Smit fec:" appears in the bottom right corner of the frame.</image-desc>

->*It rejects what weighs it down.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Gravata respuit." The term "gravata" refers to things that are heavy, burdened, or earthbound.</note>

<note>This emblem illustrates the soul's transition from earthly concerns to spiritual ones. While the geese—birds that are often earthbound and heavy—remain on the ground, the figure of Divine Love points toward the celestial light, urging the soul to cast off the "weight" of material desires.</note>

<margin>Engraved by I. Smit</margin> <note>Jan Smit (1648–1729) was a Dutch engraver known for his work on emblem books and devotional illustrations.</note>

The soul, touched by a celestial fire,
No longer seeks the mud of the earth;
It leaves behind every heavy desire
To find the path of its second birth.

See how the wings of the spirit unfold,
Refusing the chains that the world would impose;
Like the sun breaking through with its rays of gold,
The heart finds the peace that the Heavens disclose.

What use is the weight of a temporal crown,
Or the riches that keep us so close to the dust?
The wise man will cast every burden right down,
And place in the Savior his ultimate trust.

<summary>This emblem depicts Divine Love teaching the soul to reject earthly burdens—represented by the grounded geese—in favor of looking toward heaven. It emphasizes the mystical theme of "contemptus mundi" or the rejection of the world to achieve spiritual lightness.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual weight, rejection of worldliness, Jan Smit, transcendence, celestial light, emblems</keywords>

<header>OF O. VAN VEEN. 79.</header>

### XXIII.

->He disdains hearts that are weighed down.<-

Ah, let us never listen to what the flesh inspires!
Let us listen only to Jesus who speaks to our heart:
Happy is he who lives under His empire!
The pleasures of this world below have nothing but deceit:
He who follows them, follows a seducer.
The grace of Jesus gives solid pleasures;
The virtues serve as our guides;
Divine Love <note>The "Divine Cupid" or "Anima" figure common in Van Veen's emblems</note>, when you lead our steps
One does not go astray.
What innocent delights one finds in following you,
And what torments one finds in following the flesh!
Sacred fire, burn me with your celestial ardor,
Purify by burning the stains of my heart;
Let no blemish remain,
That I may carry into your bosom a soul entirely pure.

<summary>This page features a spiritual poem emphasizing the purification of the soul through Divine Love and the rejection of worldly, "fleshly" temptations. It reflects the 17th-century emblem book tradition where art and poetry combine to provide moral instruction.</summary>

### XXIV.

->*He makes one very generous.*<-

How sweet it is to give when one receives without
->ceasing!<-
The more I give, the more I am urged
To receive new gifts.
How immense Your riches are,
Divine Love <note>The author addresses God as "Divine Love," a common mystical personification in 17th-century devotional literature.</note>, since to such beautiful gifts
You even add rewards!
You pay the interest on Your own gifts, Lord;
You crown Your own blessings by crowning my merit:
If I serve You, if I please You,
If I fulfill my duties,
Is it not from You alone that I hold Your benefits?
Nevertheless, O supreme Goodness,
As if it were to myself
That You owed some return;
You overwhelm me with an immense favor;
I am beside myself when I think
Of the great excess of Your Love.

<summary>This emblem reflects on the nature of divine grace, expressing the paradox that human merit and generosity are themselves gifts from God, who rewards the very virtues He inspires.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, generosity, grace, merit, gratitude, spiritual rewards</keywords>

### XXIV.

<image-desc>
An engraving set within a rectangular frame depicting a scene of divine or allegorical charity. On the left, two child-like angels (one with a radiant halo) carry a large, shallow basket or platter filled with coins, which they are distributing. In the center, a seated woman, accompanied by a small child and an infant, reaches out her hand to receive a coin. Behind her stands a man leaning on crutches, suggesting he is lame or a beggar, and another figure in a turban-like head covering. The background shows a lush landscape with many trees and a distant building or estate on the far left. The engraver's signature "I. Smit fecit" is visible in the bottom right corner of the illustration.
</image-desc>

->He makes [one] generous.<- <note>original Latin: "Facit munificum"</note>

This illustration by the engraver J. Smit <note>Jan Smit was a 17th-century Dutch engraver known for his work on emblem books and devotional illustrations.</note> captures the essence of **charity** as a divine mandate. The "Divine Love" mentioned in the previous poem is here personified through angels who actively distribute wealth to those in need.

The presence of the **beggars** and the woman with her children emphasizes the earthly duty of the faithful: to act as a conduit for God's blessings. By receiving "interest on Your own gifts," the believer is empowered to provide **alms** <term>alms: money or food given to poor people</term> to the less fortunate, reflecting the light of the divine halo back into the world.

<summary>This page features an allegorical engraving by Jan Smit illustrating the concept of divine generosity through the act of angels distributing alms to the poor. It serves as a visual counterpart to the previous page's meditation on how God's grace inspires human charity.</summary>
<keywords>charity, alms, angels, Jan Smit, generosity, Divine Love, poverty, 17th-century engraving</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the illustration for emblem twenty-five, following the previous page's focus on Charity. It continues the allegorical journey of the Soul and Divine Love, shifting the focus to the external threats of envy and worldly destruction.</meta>

### XXV.

<image-desc>
An engraving framed by a simple border showing two small, angelic children walking through a landscape. The child on the left has a radiant halo emanating from their head and appears to be leading the other. The child on the right has wings and carries a leafy branch (possibly an olive or laurel branch). In the background, a large city is consumed by fire, with thick, dark plumes of smoke rising into the sky. A body of water with a small sailboat and a wooden bridge over a stream are also depicted. In the foreground, near the children's feet, a small serpent or snake is seen on the ground. The signature "I. Smit fec" is etched into the bottom-left corner within the frame.
</image-desc>

->Envy is the shadow of love.<- <note>original Latin: "Amoris umbra invidia"</note>

This illustration, crafted by the engraver Jan Smit <note>Jan Smit was a 17th-century Dutch artist specializing in religious and moral emblems.</note>, provides a visual meditation on the perils that accompany a virtuous life. The two **putti** <term>putti: plump, winged infant figures, often used in art to represent various forms of love or spiritual states</term> represent the Soul and Divine Love. While they walk in harmony, they are not alone.

The presence of the **serpent** at their feet is a direct reference to **invidia** <term>invidia: the Latin term for envy or jealousy, often depicted as a snake or a poisonous influence</term>. The title reminds the reader that just as a body inevitably casts a shadow, great love or divine favor inevitably attracts the "shadow" of envy from the world.

In the distance, the **burning city** serves as a stark contrast to the peaceful path of the children. It symbolizes the destructive nature of human passions and the fragility of earthly achievements when compared to the eternal peace sought by the Soul. While the world may be consumed by the "fires" of jealousy and conflict, Divine Love continues to lead the faithful safely across the bridge of life, carrying the branch of peace.

<summary>This engraving illustrates the Latin maxim that envy follows love like a shadow, depicting the Soul and Divine Love walking safely past a serpent while a worldly city burns in the distance. It warns the reader that virtue and divine favor will always provoke the jealousy of the world.</summary>

<keywords>Envy, Divine Love, Soul, Jan Smit, symbolism, serpent, burning city, putti, Latin emblems</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems by Otto Vaenius (O. Vanius), focusing on the soul's devotion to God despite worldly opposition.</meta>

# OF O. VANIUS <note>Otto van Veen, also known as Vaenius, a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens who published famous emblem books.</note>

### XXV.

->*Envy is the shadow of Love.*<-

I love you, O my God, a hundred times more than my life,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And I wish to love you always:
I see all the arrows of envy falling upon me,
But your gentle hand disarms them well.
When your divine fire took possession of my heart,
When I felt its savory flame burning,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which consumes my soul,
I immediately perceived jealous fury
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Following me just as the shadow follows the fire,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And I see it appearing everywhere:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It suddenly makes itself known
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At all times, and in all places.
As soon as pure Love <note>Refers to "Amour-propre" or Divine Love in the context of 17th-century mysticism.</note> wishes to serve as our guide,
From the moment it commands within us,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The jealous homicide <note>A personification of Envy as a murderer of the spirit.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Makes us feel its blows.
But whatever harm its fury does to me,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My JESUS, your grace
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Will be my sure support,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I fear nothing:
You are my support, your fires are my delights:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ah, can one buy this blessing
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through too many sacrifices?

L
<meta>catchword: XXVI. Nothing</meta>

<summary>This poem explores the spiritual paradox that divine love inevitably attracts worldly envy, which follows the believer like a shadow follows light. The author concludes that no amount of earthly suffering or sacrifice is too great a price for the comfort of God's grace.</summary>
<keywords>Envy, Divine Love, Jesus, Grace, Sacrifice, Spiritual Warfare, Otto Vaenius, Emblems</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### X X V I .

->*Nothing is heavy to one who loves.*<-

When one loves their God with a true love,
The harshest labors appear light to us.
How delightful is the yoke of the Lord! <note>A reference to Matthew 11:30: "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>
To please Him, one fears neither torments nor dangers.

Perfect Love cannot fear pain;
He who fears it, loves but weakly:
He who fears the yoke, who dreads the chain,
Is not a true Lover. <note>The author uses the language of courtly romance ("Amant") to describe the soul's relationship with the Divine.</note>
To suffer for what one loves
Is a charming pleasure
When Love is extreme.

Love, Love, your divine rigor
Has nothing but what is good and lovable:
How true it is that a good heart
Finds it preferable
To any other sweetness!

Sweet and pleasant labors!
Delicious burden
Setting my soul at liberty,
How you please my heart, though contrary to the senses!
Ah, grant that my martyrdom
May never end, Love, until I expire!

<sig>XXVII. L</sig>

<vocab>Love, God, Lord, labors, yoke, martyrdom, delightful, Soul</vocab>

<summary>This emblem explores the paradox of "sweet suffering," arguing that divine love renders the most difficult religious duties and physical trials effortless and even joyful. It uses the metaphor of a "delicious burden" to describe the soul's total devotion to God.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, spiritual discipline, martyrdom, joy in suffering, mysticism, the easy yoke</keywords>

### X X V I .

->*Nothing is burdensome to one who loves.*<-

When one loves their God with a true love,
The harshest labors appear light to us.
How delightful is the yoke of the Lord! <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 11:30: "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>

Perfect Love cannot fear pain;
He who fears it, loves but weakly:
He who fears the yoke, who dreads the chain,

Is a charming pleasure
When Love is extreme.

Love, Love, your divine rigor
Has nothing but what is good and lovable:
How true it is that a good heart
Finds it preferable
To any other sweetness!

Sweet and pleasant labors!
Delicious burden <note>The accompanying illustration shows Divine Love (Amor) carrying a heavy bull’s head and hide; in the symbolic language of the time, the bull represents both laborious strength and the sacrifice of one's own will.</note>

May never end, Love, until I expire!

# E M B L E M S

### X X V I I .

->*From a single Love, many blessings.*<- <note>original Latin: "Ab uno Amore multa bona."</note>

<note>The illustration by Jan Smit <gloss>I. Smit fec. / Jan Smit made this</gloss> serves as a visual allegory for the soul's progress. The two winged figures represent different aspects of Divine Love or the soul's affection. Together, they carry a "basket of virtues." Among the items are the anchor (symbolizing Hope), the mirror (symbolizing Prudence or self-examination), and the crown of thorns and crosses (symbolizing the imitation of Christ’s suffering). The message is that once a person possesses true Divine Love, all other virtues follow naturally.</note>

<term>Amor</term> <note>Love, specifically Divine or Sacred Love in this context.</note>
<term>Bona</term> <note>Good things, blessings, or spiritual fruits.</note>

<sig>XXVIII. M</sig>

<summary>This emblem illustrates Divine Love as the source of all spiritual goods, showing winged figures carrying a basket of symbolic virtues such as Hope, Prudence, and Devotion. It emphasizes that a single devotion to God brings forth a multitude of moral and spiritual strengths.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, virtues, Jan Smit, allegory, spiritual fruit, Hope, Prudence, Cross, Crown of Thorns</keywords>

# OF O. VAENIUS.

### XXVII.

->*Love alone is the source of all good.*<-

In the union of Love one finds every good,
It communicates life:
It is within these sweet bonds
Where the soul is enslaved,
That these happy Lovers
Taste a thousand contentments.

Pure Love crowns them with all the virtues;
Far from being burdened by this weight,
They find themselves laden with the favors it gives them
And relieved all at the same time.
O divine assembly,
O Happiness without equal!
Dear and sweet slavery,
Pleasant preparation! <note>The original French "apareil" can refer to both preparation and the outward display of a ceremony.</note>

Though there appear here crosses and sufferings,
Everything is filled with peace, with innocent pleasures:
Let us not stop at appearances alone,
But let us penetrate to the very inside.
See, how content this soul is!
One perceives easily in its eyes
That its entire expectation
Is already in the heavens;
That it sees only true delights
In what the worldly <note>original: "mondains" — those preoccupied with earthly rather than spiritual life.</note> call tortures.

Love, Love, give me these favors,
I prefer the cross to all sweetnesses.

<sig>L 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: XXVIII. The</meta>

<summary>This page features a poem reflecting on the paradoxical nature of divine love, where spiritual "slavery" and the "cross" of suffering are transformed into supreme joy and freedom. It emphasizes looking past outward appearances to find inner peace and heavenly anticipation.</summary>
<keywords>Otho Vaenius, divine love, spiritual union, virtue, suffering and joy, mysticism, sacred emblems</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

## XXVIII.

->The blows of Love are very sweet.<-

Love <note>In this context, "Love" refers to Divine Love or Cupid as an allegory for God's affection</note>, what must I do?
      I see you in anger:
Ah, how I fear, Love, your wrath.
It is that which I dread; alas, not your blows.
      Your coldness, your long absences
Have more harshness than all your vengeances.
   Strike, tear me apart, but do not be angry:
I love my punishment, I cherish my ordeal;
I adore your rigors, and find a thousand charms
      Even in your justice,
      I follow it step by step.
   Always for you and against myself
I support your blows with my extreme love,
      And find them charming:
Do not spare me then, my adorable Father <note>The poet addresses Love as "Father," signaling a shift from romantic allegory to a prayer to God</note>,
Let the harshest torments fall upon me:
      If you are not in anger,
      I will make them my contentment:
But if you become angry, I can no longer live:
      Gather instead all your fires,
      Make me the most unhappy of men,
      But permit me to follow you.

<meta>catchword: XXIX. The</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the paradox of divine discipline, where the narrator expresses a preference for physical or spiritual suffering over the "coldness" of God's absence or anger. It reflects the 18th-century mystical tradition of finding joy in "sweet" trials as a proof of God's presence.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual discipline, martyrdom of the heart, 18th-century mysticism, emblems, religious poetry</keywords>

->*The sweet scourge of Love*<-

      I see you in anger:

It is that which I dread; alas, not your blows.

## XXIX.

<image-desc>An engraving within a black rectangular border. Two winged cherubs are seated together on a rocky bank, jointly holding a small, upright leafy branch or sapling. The cherub on the right carries a quiver of arrows at its side. In the background, a river separates the figures from a detailed cityscape featuring various buildings, towers, and a large domed structure resembling a cathedral. ->Peace and Love dwell together in one seat.<-
<note>original Latin: *Una in sede morantur Pax & amor*</note>

<vocab>Peace, love, Jan Smit <note>A 17th-century Dutch engraver known for his work on religious and emblem books</note>, engraving, cherubs, peace, love</vocab>

<summary>This emblem illustrates the inseparable nature of Peace and Love, personified as two cherubs sharing a single seat and holding a sapling that symbolizes growth and vitality. <meta>This page continues the poetic reflections on divine love, likely part of a series based on the emblems of Otto Vaenius (O. Vanius). The tone remains mystical and devotional.</meta>

### XXIX.

->Peace and Love go together.<-

Calm and tranquility
Always accompany pure and sincere Love;
Sweet peace is necessary
To discern within ourselves holy Charity <note>The term "Charité" here refers to "Caritas," the highest form of selfless, divine love.</note>:
Trouble and grief never accompany it
In the city or in the country:
In pleasures or indeed in pain
Equanimity <note>original: "L'égalité"</note> constitutes its happiness:
Peace follows it, peace makes its delights
Even in the midst of tortures.
You had promised it well, O my divine Spouse <note>A common mystical reference to Christ as the Bridegroom of the soul.</note>,
This peace which can only proceed from you;
This peace which surpasses all good,
Which your grace produces in us,
Which the world cannot give,
A peace that it even ignores:
O my great God, whom I love and whom I adore,
I wish with all my heart to abandon myself to you.
May your peace be my wealth,
My asylum and my fortress:
It possesses a heart when you fill it.
It is; You are:
Let us be silent, it is enough.
Taste peace, my heart; tongues be mute;
And let us never speak
Of this happy Peace!

<sig>L 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: XXX. The Ef-</meta>

<summary>This page features a devotional poem exploring the inseparable nature of divine peace and love, emphasizing that true tranquility comes only from God and remains constant through both joy and suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Peace, Divine Love, Charity, Equanimity, Mysticism, Otto Vaenius, Silence</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XXX.

->*Hope nourishes a loving Soul.*<-

Hope serves as nourish-
ment
To the true Lover <note>In this mystical context, the "Lover" refers to the human soul seeking God.</note>
Amidst the labors that one
endures:
Pure Charity,
And sincere Faith
Are the holy law
Which governs life.
The soul, enraptured in God,
Finds nothing else
But the unique Good.
He alone satisfies her
And creates her pleasure;
A touching peace
Fulfills her desire.

Happy Hope
That nothing disappoints!
Since in advance
One receives here—
In the firm expectation
Of the promised happiness—
A constant soul,
A submissive spirit,
A fervent love,
An unfeigned faith,

<column-break/>

A contentment
Pure and untouched.

With great courage
This generous heart
Sees the storm break:
The foaming waves
Show the shipwreck
Painted before the eyes.
The inflexible heart
Is not moved by it:
It is no longer sensitive,
Its eye is closed <note>Literally "plugged" or "blocked," meaning the soul is blind to worldly distractions.</note>
To every other thing
Except for its Lord:
The soul rests
In His sacred heart.

Admirable Lover,
How contentedly you live
Despite the dangers!
Your pains are light,
Your wealth is immense,
Your heart is without care.
What causes all of this?
It is your Hope.

<sig>XXXI. THE L</sig>

<summary>This emblem illustrates how the theological virtues of Faith, Hope, and Charity sustain the soul through worldly storms, leading to a state of mystical peace and rest within the Sacred Heart.</summary>
<keywords>Hope, Charity, Faith, Divine Love, Sacred Heart, Mysticism, Spiritual Perseverance</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XXXI.

->*Love hates delays.*<- <note>original Latin: "Odit moras." In the context of spiritual emblems, this phrase signifies that Divine Love is characterized by an eager and swift nature that cannot endure hesitation or spiritual sloth.</note>

A detailed engraving set within a rectangular frame. Two winged <term>putti</term> <note>Winged, infant-like figures common in Baroque art, often representing spirits or personifications of love.</note> are depicted in a landscape. The figure in the foreground wears a light tunic and carries a bow, while the companion behind him raises a switch or whip as if to urge him forward with greater speed. <summary>This emblem illustrates the concept of spiritual urgency, using the contrast between winged putti and a crawling tortoise to show that the soul should pursue the Divine with zeal and without delay.</summary>

## XXXI.

### *Love hates delays.*

Divine LOVE hates all indifference;
->As soon as it takes possession of a heart<-
->It grants a holy vigor<-
->Opposed to negligence.<-

As soon as one loves truly, one becomes diligent,
->Love always makes the soul alert;<-
->One watches, one prays, one is fervent,<-
Whatever is not for God appears to us as a loss:
One never complains, no matter what must be suffered,
One believes oneself overpaid for the harshest sufferings,
->Even if it were necessary to die for them;<-
Love contains within itself all rewards.

->How different pure Love is<-
->From the slowness of indolence!<-
The faithful Lover advances with eagerness
->Wherever Providence leads him:<-
Always ready to depart, always content with everything,
Whatever happens; and whatever he undertakes,
->He surely brings it to completion,<-
->Aided by a sovereign Hand.<- <note>The "sovereign Hand" refers to divine assistance or grace.</note>

->When JESUS guides our steps,<-
Who would not run, who would not fly?
->One does not fear the precipices;<-
From one's labor, one makes one's delights;
->Finally, one runs incessantly,<-
Then rest lasts eternally.

<summary>This emblem explores the theme of spiritual diligence, arguing that true divine love removes sloth and inspires the soul to act with speed, joy, and endurance under God's guidance.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, diligence, spiritual fervor, Providence, Jesus, perseverance, overcoming sloth</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
EMBLEMS

### XXXII.

#### *Love sets all things right.*

Whatever faults our conduct may have had,
Love makes everything right and orderly again:
Nothing can ever equal
The blessing of a pure soul instructed by Love.

Lies and error never accompany
A heart guided by Charity <note>The author uses "Charity" (Caritas) here as a synonym for Divine Love.</note>:
Uprightness and peace,
Solid Humility,
Prevent the detours that are the fruits of vanity;
Candor, sincerity,
Good faith, joy, and innocence,
Are the sound science
That pure Love teaches to its Lovers:

"If you are not," he says, "even as Children,
"You would not know how to please me:
"They know how to praise me, love me, and satisfy me,
"I take pleasure in their hearts,
"And I create their happiness.
"It is not to the Sages of the world
"That I reveal my secrets:
"It is the profound humility of little Children
"Which penetrates my holy decrees." <note>A reference to the Gospel passage: "Unless you change and become like little children, you will never enter the kingdom of heaven."</note>

How lovely is smallness!
What sweetness and attractions it possesses!
How hateful is cunning!
One sees nothing there but detours, labyrinths, and snares.

He who deceives best is considered the wisest;
He who takes the most advantage of his neighbor
Is considered clever, full of wit, and very fortunate.
But who are the most content: the children, or them?

---

<sig>d</sig>
<meta>catchword: XXXIII.</meta>

<summary>This emblem poem argues that divine love and childlike humility are superior to worldly wisdom and cunning. It emphasizes that true spiritual "science" is found in innocence and sincerity rather than the complex deceptions of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Humility, Childhood, Spiritual Innocence, Virtue vs. Cunning, Wisdom</keywords>

A radiant glow emanates from the seated child's head, suggesting a holy or enlightened state. In the background, large windows and classical columns suggest a grand, orderly setting. Scattered on the floor are a bow and a quiver of arrows—the traditional attributes of Cupid—now set aside in favor of the tools of "rectification" or alignment. ### XXXIII.

<note>Illustration: This copperplate engraving by the artist Jan Smit depicts a rugged, symbolic landscape where the soul's work is performed. ## XXXIII.

->*He prepares the way to God.*<-

Jesus is the Way, the Truth, and the Life; <note>A reference to John 14:6.</note>
Whoever follows him has found the path and the place
Which, despite the Demons and their mortal envy,
Leads us surely and conducts us to God.

He who follows JESUS walks in his light,
He serves him as a torch in the darkest night,
He even grants his heart every complete grace
Since, in leading him, he reassures and instructs him.

Although this beautiful path appears full of thorns
It is nevertheless easy, and entirely filled with flowers:
How sweet it is to walk in the divine routes!
Our own hearts and minds are but deceptive guides.

O my JESUS, without you I would not know how to follow you;
Give me then your hand and guide my steps:
Your divine hand delivers us from snares:
With such a support I shall not tremble.

Following you, I fear neither abysses nor precipices:
I wish to show you the excess of my Love,
By enduring for you the most hideous tortures
I would lose the light of day without any grief.

<sig>M</sig>
<meta>catchword: XXXIV. All</meta>

<summary>This page features a devotional poem reflecting on Jesus as the essential guide and "Way" to God, emphasizing that divine grace makes the difficult path of virtue easy to tread. It concludes with a declaration of martyr-like devotion, where the speaker expresses a willingness to suffer for their faith.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, The Way, spiritual guidance, divine light, grace, martyrdom, Otto Vaenius, emblems, devotion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, focusing on the cyclical nature of divine grace and the necessity of Love (Charity) for spiritual fruitfulness.</meta>

# E M B L E M S

### X X X I V.

->*Everything must return to its primary source.*<-

How magnificent and great is your liberality,
O Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) to refer to Divine Love or the Holy Spirit</note>;
Its noble and beautiful quality
Is to desire that we ask of you!
But when you give, you desire a return;
Permit me this word, divine Love,
It seems that a little self-interest animates you;
You give the virtues, and you want their fruits:
But could one rightly refuse them to you without crime,
Since by your Love you have produced them?

Virtue without Love is a sterile tree;
Love makes everything fertile:
Though it is entirely fire, it differs in this point
From the fire one sees in the material world,
Whose heat, far from being fruitful,
Destroys and consumes everything, and reproduces nothing.

The sacred fire in our heart
Gives birth
To the good seed,
Makes it grow and ripen by its celestial ardor.

O divine fire, who produces all things,
Faith, who gives to everything a just value,
You are no less the end than the admirable cause
Of eternal happiness.

What hope,
What abundance,
What sweetness!
Chaste delights,
Happy torments <note>original: "Heureux suplices" — a common mystical paradox where the "pain" of longing for God is considered a joy</note>,
O holy Love
What an eternal dwelling place there shall be!

<sig>XXXV.</sig>
<vocab>liberality, Love, virtue, interest, sterile, fertile, sacred fire, Faith, happiness, hope</vocab>

<summary>This emblem explores the theological concept that all virtues originate from Divine Love and must be returned to God as spiritual fruit. It contrasts the destructive nature of worldly passion with the creative, life-giving "sacred fire" of divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual fruitfulness, sacred fire, virtue vs. grace, mystical theology, reciprocity with God</keywords>

### X X X I V.

How magnificent and great is your generosity,
O Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) to refer to Divine Love or the Holy Spirit</note>;

You give the virtues, and you want their fruits:
But could one rightly refuse them to you without it being a crime,
Since by your Love you have produced them?

Virtue without Love is a barren tree;
Love makes everything fertile:
Though it is entirely fire, it differs in this point
From the fire one sees in the material world,
Whose heat, far from being fruitful,
Destroys and consumes everything, and reproduces nothing.

The sacred fire in our heart
Gives birth
To the good seed,

# OF O. VAENIUS. 91

### XXXV.

->*He is firm and constant.*<-

Love, in your presence the harshest torments
    Are taken for contentment;
Tortures and fires prove my constancy:
    Sustained by your powerful arm,
    This unique assistance,
This infinite happiness of seeing you so present,
Takes from me the feeling of the most frightful pains;
    The executioners armed with their racks <note>The original French "gênes" refers specifically to instruments of torture used to extract confessions.</note>
    Have much more horror than I
    At my excessive suffering.

    Love, source of my delights,
Do not abandon me in the midst of these tortures:
    If you abandoned me, alas!
    Love, what would I not fear?
    Sustained by your powerful hand,
    How easy it is for the soul to be constant!
Ah, I would soon be overwhelmed with terror,
O how weak I would be and how much fear I would have
If you abandoned me for a moment to myself!
When you sustain me by your supreme grace,
I no longer know myself; I am victorious
    Over these furious enemies:
    If I succumb in appearance,
It is to make your power shine forth before their eyes.

<sig>M 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: XXXVI. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page features a poetic meditation on the power of Divine Love to sustain a person through physical torture and suffering, emphasizing that constancy comes from grace rather than personal strength. It is part of a series of emblems by Otho Vaenius regarding the "Amoris Divini" or Divine Love.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, constancy, martyrdom, torture, Otho Vaenius, spiritual strength, grace, endurance</keywords>

<header>E M B L E M S</header>

## XXXVI.

### Love edifies & builds.

O How Divine Love is a good Architect!
He builds in our hearts a lovely dwelling,
->Consecrated for Love.<-
It is there that one serves Him, loves Him & respects Him.
It is in the depths of the heart that God makes His
dwelling,
He builds it, He founds it, He adorns it, He beautifies it,
->He comes there at every hour,<-
->He carves, He prunes, He polishes.<- <note>The author uses architectural and gardening metaphors to describe the "stripping away" of the ego to make room for God.</note>
->He spares neither care nor pain:<-
O how happy is the man when with an eye of faith,
He contemplates in peace the sovereign Goodness,
That he dies perfectly to live for the divine King!
Revived by the same, he sees springing up within himself
The living water, as revealed to the Samaritan woman. <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 4:10-14, where Jesus offers "living water" to a woman at a well.</note>
->Yes, the interior man<- <term>interior man: a mystical term referring to the soul or the spiritual self as opposed to the physical body.</term>
->Finds then in his heart<-
->This living fountain:<-
->It is there that in truth<-
->He adores the Father;<-
And already his spirit, placed in Eternity,
->No longer clings to the earth.<-
Make then, O my God, of my heart your
temple:
->Then, despite every storm & every noise,<-
->I shall have the calm of the night,<-
And nothing shall prevent me from contemplating You.

<meta>Continuity: The next emblem (XXXVII) begins with the word "He".</meta>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of an architect to describe how Divine Love builds a spiritual temple within the human heart. It emphasizes the mystical "interior life" where the soul finds peace and living water by detaching from earthly concerns.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual architecture, interior man, Samaritan woman, living water, heart as temple, mystical union</keywords>

# OF O. VANIUS.

### XXXVII.

->*He spreads a charming fragrance.*<-

Draw me, my God, my only hope,
By your so precious perfumes:
Already I felt myself falling into a faint,
But this delicious balm,
Strengthening my heart, gives it the courage
To run after you, to run after you in all places:
I do not desire to have any other portion
On the earth nor in the heavens.

Withdraw, sweetnesses, pleasures, favors, caresses;
O God, it is you alone whom I want,
You are all my good, my strength, my riches,
You alone can make me happy.

I feel that this perfume is of an extreme strength,
I know well how to discern its scent:
But, O divine Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride.</note> whom I adore & whom I love,
You alone are sufficient for my heart.

To leave you for a moment to taste your delights
And to look at them apart from you,
That would be for me a rigorous torture,
Everything is bitter for me, you alone appear sweet,
You alone appear to me lovable,
You alone fulfill all my desires.
Is there any delectable object without you?
In you are enclosed the solid pleasures.

Since you are sufficient, my Lord, unto yourself,
For whom would you not be sufficient?
You mix your kindness with supreme grandeur:
For whom would you lack charms?

<vocab>God, hope, perfume, balm, heart, Spouse, Lord, pleasures, charms</vocab>

<summary>A poetic meditation on the divine fragrance of God, expressing the soul's desire to forsake worldly pleasures to follow the "Divine Spouse." It emphasizes that God alone is the source of true strength and satisfaction.</summary>
<keywords>mysticism, divine love, Song of Solomon, spiritual fragrance, Otto Vaenius, devotion, soul's journey</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, focusing on the soul's security and courage when united with Divine Love.</meta>

# E M B L E M S

### XXXVIII.

->*With Love one is in safety:*<-

How I laugh at your effort!
I do not fear death at all,
Near my Well-beloved I am in safety:
You could not make me lose my confidence:
Bring near, bring near your chains and your irons,
I have only contempt for your various torments.

When Divine Love <note>original: "l’Amour divin"</note> takes possession of our soul,
And when it makes us feel its favoring flame,
Which consumes within us all self-interest <note>original: "proprieté" - here meaning the sense of private ownership or attachment to the self</note>,
Released from this **s e l f** one lives in liberty,
Chains and prisons could not cause fear:
The sword cannot reach us:

Could I be frightened by the horror of passing away?
Death has for my heart a thousand secret charms:
It may well take from me a fragile life;
But this loss is followed by a sovereign happiness,
Since I must fall most infallibly
Into the arms of my Lover <note>The "Lover" or "Amant" refers to Christ or the Divine, a common metaphor in 17th-century mystical poetry</note>.
Ah, does one fear to see what one loves?
Whatever it costs, extreme Love
Finds every price too low
To enjoy forever his divine charms.

When Charity <note>original: "la Charité" - referring to the theological concept of selfless, divine love</note> takes possession of our heart,
Hunger, nakedness, nothing separates us from it,
Death, even hell, or persecution,
Could not prevent this holy union.

<sig>XXXIX. l</sig>

<vocab>Divine Love, Well-beloved, liberty, Charity, holy union, passing away/death</vocab>

<summary>This emblem illustrates the soul's total defiance of death and physical suffering when possessed by Divine Love. It emphasizes that the destruction of the "self" leads to a spiritual liberty that welcomes death as a gateway to union with the Beloved.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, martyrdom, mysticism, spiritual liberty, union with God, contempt for death, Charity</keywords>

# XXXIX.

->He quenches the thirst.<- <note>original Latin: "Sitim extinguit"</note>

<vocab>Thirst, quenches, Love, well, pulley, Jan Smit</vocab>

<note>This emblem depicts Divine Love—represented by the winged figure of Cupid—laboring at a well. In the visual language of 17th-century spirituality, the "well" often represents the Word of God or the fountain of Grace, while the "thirst" represents the human soul's restless desire for fulfillment that can only be satisfied by the divine. The presence of a church or monastery in the background suggests that this "water" is found through religious devotion and the contemplative life.</note>

<summary>This emblem illustrates Divine Love quenching the soul's spiritual thirst by drawing water from a well, symbolizing the arrival of divine grace. It suggests that the longings of the heart are only satisfied through the labor and presence of the Divine.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual thirst, Divine Love, well of grace, Jan Smit, quenching, soul's longing, devotional art</keywords>

# BY OTTO VAENIUS

### XXXIX.

->*He quenches the thirst of the heart.*<-

Delights of the spirit, you are preferable
To the false pleasures of the senses;
They are but appearances,
You are true;
You possess substance, while they are all deceptive.

Divine truth, which all the world ignores,
You fill my heart with a celestial ardor:
Source of all my blessings, dear Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Soul as the Bride in mystical literature.</note> whom I adore,
Your healing waters flow within my heart.

May this sacred river spring up in my soul;
May these gushing waters of Divinity
Extinguish forever within me every other flame
Save that of the love of your Truth.

This entirely celestial water has the signal advantage
Of extinguishing in our hearts all kinds of fires;
But the fire of Love burns all the more from it,
The water makes it more ardent, more pure, more luminous.

Give me of this water which preserves life;
But let its effect be to cause my death:
The bonds of this body, holding me enslaved,
Prevent me from joining you and taking flight.

My soul is even more a prisoner than my body;
You alone, my Lord, can break its bonds.
Ah, let my body return to the dust,
Give to my spirit the true blessings!

<sig>XL. Who</sig>

<vocab>Delights, spirit, truth, Spouse, Divinity, Love, celestial water, Otto Vaenius</vocab>

<summary>This emblem explores the mystical paradox where divine water quenches worldly desires while fueling the fire of spiritual love. The author expresses a longing for the soul to be released from the "prison" of the physical body to achieve union with the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, spiritual marriage, divine truth, living water, asceticism, soul's journey, mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, focusing on the paradox of finding true freedom through divine submission. It follows the previous emblem's themes of internal devotion.</meta>

<header>E M B L E M S</header>

# X L.

-> *He who wishes to love is no longer free in his own way.* <-

How I love your yoke, how sweet and pleasant it is,
How vainly I used to fear it!
I am free, far from being a slave,
When I bear it while loving you.
How happy my soul is, being your captive!
I have found my liberty there.
Grant then, O Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) as a name for God or the Divine Presence</note>, that I may live
In humble dependence upon your will.

O happy yoke, which far from imprisoning my soul,
Causes a vast delight,
How well you accord with the sweet flame
That I keep in my heart like a precious gift!

The world, which sees only the apparent burden
By which I seem overwhelmed in its eyes,
Believes me very miserable: but my heart is at 
liberty;
Far from being a slave, it is filled with delights.

No, the world hardly understands
Despite so many labors, the happiness within;
Valuing only that which prospers,
Honors, pleasures, and that which flatters the senses.

The children of JESUS have much more wisdom,
Valuing nothing, tasting only the cross:
Ah, what delicacy their taste possesses,
To know how to make such a good choice!

I yield to you, worldly ones, the honors and delights;
I love all my labors, my chain, my prison:
Even if I had to suffer every torment,
I would still find that I have great reason:
Let us say without artifice,
That he who knows Love and its true value,
And who knows how to do it justice,
Will approve of the inclination of my heart.

<meta>catchword: XLI. The Uni-</meta>

<summary>Emblem XL explores the spiritual paradox where submitting to the "yoke" of divine love results in true freedom rather than slavery. The text contrasts the internal joy of the believer with the world's superficial perception of religious life as a burden.</summary>

<keywords>divine love, spiritual liberty, the yoke of Christ, internal happiness, worldly vanity, devotion, mysticism</keywords>

# X L.

-> *No one shall be free who wishes to love.* <note>original: "Nullus liber erit si quis amare volet." This is a quote from the Roman poet Ovid, here repurposed to explain that love—whether earthly or divine—always demands the surrender of one's independence.</note> <-

## Page 41.

***

->*Love shines above all Virtues.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Micat inter omnes Amor Virtutes." This motto emphasizes that Love—specifically Divine Love—is the most brilliant and essential of the virtues, outshining others like Justice or Piety.</note>

***

### Illustration: The Preeminence of Divine Love

*Description of the Engraving:*
This rectangular copperplate engraving depicts a complex allegorical scene. In the center, a radiant child figure representing **Divine Love** (or the Christ Child) is enthroned. He holds a **palm branch** <note>a symbol of victory over death and the world</note> in his right hand, while resting his left hand upon the head of another seated child who has his arms crossed over his chest in a gesture of humble submission, representing the **Human Soul**.

Above the central figure, a hand emerges from heavy clouds, holding a circular wreath or **crown of thorns** <note>This serves as a reminder that Divine Love is inextricably linked to the Passion and sacrifice</note>. 

To the left stands a veiled woman with hands joined in prayer next to a cylindrical pedestal topped with a cross, personifying **Faith or Piety**. To the right stands a woman holding a balance scale, the personification of **Justice**. Other figures look on from the background, witnessing the scene. In the bottom-left corner, the signature of the artist appears: "I. Smit fecit" <note>Latin: "Jan Smit made this." Jan Smit was a known 17th-century engraver.</note>.

***

**Key Vocabulary:**
**Love**, **Virtues**, **Shines**, **Jan Smit**, **allegory**, **Justice**, **palm branch**.

# DO. VÆNIUS. <note>Latin: Dominicus Vænius, the Latinized name of the artist Otto van Veen.</note>

### X L I.

->*The Unique Love shines among the virtues.*<-

Love, divine Love, who encompasses within yourself
The supreme excellence of all the virtues,
Source of justice and support of faith,
Everything that one hopes for is enclosed within you.

Without you, penance is a hypocrisy,
Prudence and strength a pure mania;
Without you, divine Love, crosses, martyrdoms, and torments,
Would be but vain amusements.

It is therefore sacred Love that regulates all things;
It is the goal proposed to us,
It gives to all goods their price and value,
Everything would be languishing without its noble vigor.
It makes that which crawled upon the earth fly to heaven,
It brings into our hearts both peace and war;
It is always through its care that one is victorious:
It rights our steps, it opens our eyes.

How unhappy one would be without its sweet assistance!
It is in our labors our unique hope,
In our afflictions it is our recourse.
Sacred Love, rule and conduct my days
By the order of your Providence;
I wish to live and die under your dependence!

---
<sig>N</sig>
<meta>catchword: XLII. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page features Emblem 41 from Otto van Veen’s work, presenting a poetic meditation on Divine Love as the essential foundation for all other virtues and religious acts. It argues that without Love, even martyrdom and penance are meaningless, positioning Love as the guiding force of Providence.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, virtues, Otto van Veen, Providence, justice, faith, moral emblems, 17th-century spirituality</keywords>

# E M B L E M S.

## XLII.

->*Love overcomes all.*<-

Who can resist Love?
He who overcomes everything, whose invincible strength
Despite forts and ramparts, pierces, breaks, and makes a way,
Reaching that which appears most inaccessible.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;God yields to our strong ardor,
He suspends his wrath, is appeased and lays down his arms
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When he discovers in the depths of our heart
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That Love is the source of our tears.
Love, powerful Love and sovereign victor,
How charming are your blows! How I love your wounds!
Shoot, cut, destroy, do not spare my breast,
Make, make my blood flow through a hundred thousand openings,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Leave nothing that is not entirely divine,
Remove impurity, cleanse the filth,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Banish what remains of the human,
You desire for your children souls that are entirely pure.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You only destroy a heart to make it strong:
When it no longer belongs to itself, God moves and animates it;
He achieves everything without making any effort:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This figure <note>Referring to the engraving that would have accompanied this emblem in the original printed book.</note> expresses to us
How divine Love guides the bow and the arm
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of this fortunate Lover;
See how skillfully and without any embarrassment
She shoots her arrow to conquer destiny:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She pierces with the first blow
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This thick and strong breastplate:
No, there is nothing that cannot be accomplished
Aided by Love, for its strength surpasses
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The harshest effort of Hell,
Finally, Love is stronger than death. <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon 8:6, "For love is strong as death."</note>

XLIII. Acti-

<summary>This emblem illustrates the omnipotence of Divine Love, depicting it as an archer capable of piercing any defense and purifying the soul through sacred suffering. It concludes with the theological triumph of love over both hell and death.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual purification, mysticism, spiritual warfare, victory over death, sacred wounds</keywords>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This figure <note>This refers to the accompanying engraving by the artist J. Smit, which depicts a winged figure of Divine Love shooting an arrow at a target on a piece of armor.</note> expresses to us
How divine Love guides the bow and the arm
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of this fortunate <term>Lover</term>; <note>In the context of 17th-century devotional literature, the "Lover" represents the human soul in a mystical relationship with God.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She pierces with the first blow
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This thick and strong breastplate:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The harshest effort of Hell,
Finally, Love is stronger than death. <note>A reference to the biblical Song of Solomon 8:6: "Set me as a seal upon thine heart... for love is strong as death."</note>

<note>original signature: n.</note>

<summary>This emblem illustrates the irresistible power of Divine Love, which is capable of piercing even the most hardened hearts and defensive spiritual "armor." It frames the soul's surrender to God as a restorative destruction that removes human impurity to make room for the divine.</summary>

# EMBLEMS

## XLIII.

->*Shaken, it grows stronger.*<- <note>original Latin: "Agitatus fortior." This motto draws on a common botanical metaphor in 17th-century thought: a tree's roots are said to strike deeper into the earth the more its branches are tossed by the wind.</note>

<note>This page is an illustration by the artist Jan Smit <note>original: "I. Smit fec:", Latin for "J. Smit made this." Jan Smit was a Dutch engraver active in the late 17th century, frequently collaborating on emblem books.</note>. The engraving depicts two winged children—known as <term>putti</term> <note>Italian for "little boys"; in this context, they are used as allegorical figures for Divine Love and the human Soul.</note>—at the base of a massive tree. One figure, representing the Soul, clings to the trunk while the second figure, distinguished by a radiant halo representing Divine Love, offers a supportive embrace. In the upper corner, a personified wind blows a powerful gust toward the canopy, testing the tree's strength. A discarded bow lies on the ground, signaling that the initial "wounding" of the soul by Love's arrows (the subject of the previous page) has passed, and the current stage of spiritual life is one of endurance and growth through hardship.</note>

<summary>This emblem illustrates the concept that spiritual trials do not destroy the soul but rather strengthen its resolve and its bond with Divine Love. The imagery of a tree being buffeted by wind serves as a metaphor for the fortifying power of adversity.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Love, Jan Smit, putti, spiritual trial, resilience, emblem, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 318 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems based on the works of Otto van Veen (O. Vaenius), specifically focusing on the soul's resilience through divine support.</meta>

# XLIII.

->Agitated, it becomes firmer.<-

The more I am agitated, the more strength I feel;
   The storm serves only to better steady me;
Since my dear Spouse <note>In mystical tradition, the "Spouse" refers to Christ or the Divine, following the imagery of the Song of Solomon.</note> deigns to sustain me,
   Evils touch only the surface.
The more afflictions I have, the more I experience within
A sense of peace and sweetness: Sovereign Goodness,
   In exchange for a mere appearance of pain,
   Overwhelms me with contentment.
Come and strike me, all the arrows of envy,
   I laugh at your vain efforts:
      The most painful life
      And the harshest deaths,
Are an infinite source of infinite blessings,
   And through the storm, one is led to the harbor.
   Ah, how ravished a soul is then!
   How it then blesses its fate!
      God repays with interest
      A brief sorrow,
      Giving Himself without measure
To whoever, for His sake, despises a brief and vain happiness.
Holy sweetness of heaven, pleasant ideas,
You fill the heart that wishes to receive you;
By your powerful attractions, possessed souls
   Do not allow themselves to be moved.
Neither the pleasures of the senses, nor frivolous fears,
   Can shake their heart;
This noble memory with which they are imprinted,
Creating their firmness, creates also their happiness.

<summary>This page features a spiritual poem illustrating how divine love strengthens the soul against worldly afflictions and storms. It emphasizes that earthly suffering is superficial and temporary compared to the infinite peace granted by God.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual resilience, divine spouse, mysticism, Otto van Veen, emblems, peace through suffering, divine providence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems, following the themes of divine devotion and the soul's surrender to God.</meta>

<header>E M B L E M S</header>

### X L I V.

->*True Love knows no measure.*<-

Divine Love must be without measure;
      One never fails
      In its divine excesses:
The more violent it is, the longer its strength endures.
When one loves well, one no longer desires a rule <note>The author suggests that formal religious rules or "measures" are surpassed by the intensity of direct mystical love.</note>
      Simple Charity <term>Charity: from the Latin 'caritas', meaning selfless, divine love rather than just almsgiving.</term>
      Joined to Truth
      Takes flight like an eagle,
      Leaving behind everything that is not God,
One wants nothing of anything that acts as a middle ground.
      Ah, when Love is extreme,
      One dies to everything as well as to oneself
And one finds life in this happy death. <note>A reference to the "mystical death," where the ego is surrendered to find union with the Divine.</note>
      Ah, let us always die in this way!
The more sincere and strong our Charity shall be,
      The swifter its effort will be.
Love, by shattering everything, break the thread of my life
      How happy my fate will be,
When by its attraction, Love, powerful and strong,
      Will have snatched it from me without pity.
      Love, Love, nothing limited anymore,
      Engulf me within your Charity.

<meta>catchword: XLV.</meta>

### XLV.

*An engraving within a rectangular border depicts an allegorical scene of spiritual fortification. Two winged cherubs stand on either side of a large, blazing bonfire. The cherub on the right, representing Divine Love, is distinguished by a radiant halo and a quiver on his back; his bow lies nearby on the ground, suggesting the hunt for souls is momentarily paused to tend the fire. Above the scene, two celestial personifications of the wind emerge from the clouds, blowing powerful streams of air down onto the fire.
